What the Floods Say About Xi Jinping
2026-09-03

What the Floods Say About Xi Jinping

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
When the Yangtze River flooded in 1998 and inundated villages in central China, Jiang Zemin, the Communist Party’s general secretary, was on the scene, bullhorn in hand, to encourage local relief efforts. Ten years later, his successor, Hu Jintao, stood among the rubble after an earthquake rocked Sichuan province, in the country’s southwest. Last week, after floodwaters caused by a collapsing glacier tore through the Himalayas between Nepal and China, leaving more than 1,100 people dead and thousands more missing, the party’s current general secretary, Xi Jinping, stayed largely and safely in Beijing. In the United States, where politicians have to worry about the needs and feelings of voters, elected representatives are expected to be more visible and engaged in national crises. In 2021, Republican Senator Ted Cruz earned public condemnation when he was caught absconding to Mexico while Texas suffered a deep freeze and power outages. As a Communist dictator, Xi doesn’t have to fret over public-approval ratings. Yet his absence signals a bigger problem for China: Xi has become so powerful that he has lost touch with the country he governs. Despite being professed Marxists, China’s leaders rarely interact with the working class. But Xi at least used to try to seem like a man of the people. In 2013, about a year into his tenure, Chinese media made a big deal out of his visit to a steamed-bun restaurant in Beijing; the state-owned China Daily praised him for showing his “common touch.” Today, there’s nothing common about Xi Jinping. He has amassed greater personal power than any other Chinese leader since Mao Zedong, the Communist regime’s founder, who died half a century ago. To justify his outsize role in China’s government, Xi’s tireless propagandists portray him as something grander than a party boss: a philosopher king, whose doctrine of wisdom, called Xi Jinping Thought, is meant to be both studied and obeyed. He is the great global statesman, guiding foreign policy into a new era of peace and prosperity. His various “global initiatives” outline his vision for a reformed world order. View: The aftermath of Nepal’s flash floods Mere mortals such as Jiang and Hu had to stomp around muddy disaster zones to prove their worth. But Xi “does not need to go to steam-bun shops to pretend he is with the people any longer, or, for that matter, to be visible when a major disaster has happened,” Steve Tsang, the director of the SOAS China Institute at the University of London, told me. “He no doubt thinks and therefore acts as if he is above all.” In Xi’s calculus, the Himalayan communities destroyed by the floods are hardly worthy of his attention. The area in question is part of Tibet, a region that borders Nepal and was once independent. Xi has demonstrated a persistent hostility toward China’s minorities, who, in their stubborn resistance to Chinese civilization, threaten his vision of national unity. A new law that took effect in July aims to transform Tibetans, Uyghurs, Mongolians, and other minorities into properly loyal citizens by forcing them to adopt the majority Han Chinese culture. Until the Tibetans fall in line, “there is not really a compelling reason for him to visit them just because a disaster struck,” Tsang argued. From Xi’s perspective, appearing at the scene of the floods would have involved more risk than reward. A purportedly all-knowing and all-seeing leader can’t make mistakes or even be associated with bad news, so Xi has good reason to avoid potentially tarnishing himself with an uncertain rescue operation. The politically safer option involves managing the crisis from afar while dispatching others to get their hands dirty. Beijing is also carefully controlling the information coming from the disaster zone. International journalists are already barred from Tibet, so any news about the disaster and the relief effort is from Chinese state sources. The state-run news agency Xinhua duly announced on Friday that Xi chaired a meeting of the party’s powerful Politburo to “deliberate and make arrangements on rescue and relief efforts.” The China-focused research firm Trivium noted that Xi’s response to the floods was in keeping with his usual approach to disasters, which is to visit long after the crisis has passed and the rebuilding has begun. Xi is “keeping himself above operational risk” and is focused on “owning the recovery rather than the chaos,” Trivium’s report said. Xi may at some point decide to show up on the ground, but probably well after the initial chaos has subsided. The stories and images from the floods have moved hearts, but Xi has bigger things to think about than a mudslide on the far edge of his empire. One of his priorities has been to reshape Chinese culture by merging the party’s beloved Marxism with traditional Confucianism. Although that effort appears to have had little traction with the public, Xi himself seems to have taken these ideas to heart. He may speak like a socialist, but he governs more like an emperor, deserving of national veneration and shielded from scrutiny. But many emperors were remote figures, removed from the real world behind the walls of the Forbidden City, and reliant on courtiers for news from outside. As Xi’s absence from the flooding disaster demonstrates, he, too, has become detached from the daily struggles and sentiments of his citizens—and unresponsive to the nation’s mounting economic and social problems as a result. How the Chinese people feel about Xi’s response is impossible to know. Assiduous censors deter candor and cleanse social-media platforms of any direct criticism. Perhaps life in an authoritarian political system breeds a practical indifference, given that people have no influence over who rules them anyway. But with a spare few hours on the ground among those who just lost everything, Xi could have at least shown that he cares. His loyal public has surely noticed that even that was too much.
The Atlantic
Should We Be Afraid of Our Food?
2026-09-03

Should We Be Afraid of Our Food?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
The cyclosporiasis outbreak that has garnered so much attention in recent months is unquestionably bad news. People have been sickened in at least 48 states, and the number of lab-confirmed cases—about 17,000—represents only a fraction of the number of people who have been infected by the parasite. But as foodborne illnesses go, a cyclospora infection—most likely caused in this instance by the consumption of iceberg lettuce contaminated with human feces—is relatively mild: unpleasant but rarely life-threatening. The two people who have died during the current outbreak had serious underlying conditions. Other foodborne pathogens are far more dangerous. More than 1 million Americans are sickened by Salmonella every year; invasive, potentially life- threatening infections occur in about 8 percent of lab-confirmed cases, and cause meningitis, sepsis, and other serious complications. A botulism infection can cause paralysis, seizures, nerve damage, lung damage, and brain damage, as well as death. A few years ago, I reported here on an outbreak of Cronobacter infections linked to powdered infant formula and the subsequent recall by Abbott Nutrition. Two recent botulism outbreaks linked to powdered infant formula made by two other companies have thus far received little attention. During an outbreak that peaked in November 2025, at least 48 infants across 17 states were stricken with botulism traced to powdered formula from the company ByHeart. The infants were hospitalized with severe complications; one of them suffered multiple strokes. Then, earlier this year, at least four infants in three states were stricken with botulism from powdered formula made by the company Nara Organics. All four infants were hospitalized. Read: The worst part of the cyclosporiasis response A key ingredient in the tainted formula sold by both companies was milk powder from a common supplier—the likely carrier of the botulism spores. Today’s powdered formula is highly industrialized, with dozens of ingredients from multiple sources. The contents of a single can of formula may contain milk from thousands of different cows. Nobody has ever fed babies this kind of stuff before. From December 2025 through February 2026, more than 100 infants in at least 10 countries were sickened in all likelihood by powdered formula tainted with spores from the bacterium Bacillus cereus. The formula, manufactured by several different companies, including Nestlé and Danone, had been contaminated by a fatty-acid additive supplied by a biotech firm in Wuhan, China. A generation ago, an outbreak of food poisoning generally remained small-scale. The source was likely to be tainted ground beef from a local butcher shop or an egg salad gone bad at a church picnic. As the scale of industrial food production has grown, so has the scale of foodborne outbreaks. When I wrote my book Fast Food Nation, published in 2001, high levels of fecal contamination of ground beef were already a nationwide problem, not just a local one, because huge ground-beef-processing plants with poor safety procedures were supplying the fast-food industry. The dairy industry now presents similar problems of scale. The supply chain for the botulism-tainted infant formula went like this: 55 dairies in California sold raw milk to a company called Organic West; that raw milk was then shipped from California to an enormous plant operated by Dairy Farmers of America in Fallon, Nevada, and processed into milk powder. For ByHeart, the powder was shipped to a plant in Iowa, turned into formula, shipped to Oregon for additional processing, packaged there, and sold throughout the United States. For Nara Organics, the milk powder was shipped from Nevada to Germany, manufactured into formula, shipped to Denmark, packaged, and shipped back to the U.S. Government never adapts quickly, and because policies are adopted piecemeal over decades, many federal food-safety rules make little sense. For instance, frozen cheese pizzas are regulated by the FDA—but if a frozen pizza has a lot of pepperoni or other meats, it’s regulated by the Department of Agriculture. The U.S. is the only developed country that divides responsibility for food safety among separate government agencies. So it is hardly surprising that monitoring and enforcement have not kept up with changes in food production. The larger problem is that the food-safety system we have—underfunded for decades—is being methodically dismantled by the Trump administration. The FDA is responsible for the safety of about 80 percent of the food consumed in the United States. Last year, James Jones, the highly respected head of the FDA’s human-foods division, resigned from his post, citing the “indiscriminate” firing of 89 staff members. The loss of such expertise threatens to cripple the agency’s food-safety efforts. Kyle Diamantas—who replaced Jones and now serves as the acting head of the FDA—has no scientific, medical, or public-health training. He is an attorney (and Donald Trump Jr.’s hunting companion). While employed at the law firm Jones Day, Diamantas defended Abbott Laboratories in a lawsuit alleging that the company had knowingly sold infant formula that could cause severe bowel problems. Abbott lost the suit and in August agreed to pay a $670 million settlement to resolve that case and other claims. In a statement to investors, Abbott said that the settlement is “not in any way an admission of liability” and that the company “remains confident in the safety of these products.” The USDA, which has food-safety oversight of meat, eggs, and poultry, has also suffered significant cutbacks under Donald Trump, losing about one-fifth of its workforce in 2025. At the CDC, the chief food-safety expert, Daniel Jernigan, resigned in August 2025, angered by budget cuts and political interference. The number of pathogens tracked by FoodNet, a crucial CDC surveillance program, has been cut by 75 percent. The amount of money spent on FoodNet annually is a fraction of what has already been spent to renovate the Reflecting Pool in front of the Lincoln Memorial. During the late 1890s, tainted and adulterated food was commonplace in the United States. Upton Sinclair’s muckraking 1906 novel, The Jungle, exposed a variety of noxious practices—such as making sausage from rats—and prompted President Theodore Roosevelt to support food-safety legislation. The food industry vehemently opposed Roosevelt: “We are paying all we care to pay,” one executive told Congress. The industry lost that battle—federal agencies gained regulatory power—but continues to fight back. It spends more on lobbying and political donations than the oil or defense industries do. The tragedy is that public-health experts agree on many of the straightforward steps that should be taken to ensure food safety. And they can point to a record of success. In 2002, a year after the publication of Fast Food Nation, I met a woman named Barbara Kowalcyk, whose 2-year-old son, Kevin, had recently died after being infected with E. coli O157:H7 from a tainted hamburger.  It was an all-too-frequent occurrence back then—nationwide outbreaks of E. coli O157:H7 were routinely caused by ground beef. Kowalcyk was determined that other parents should never have to endure her experience. Trained as a biostatistician, she returned to school and earned a doctorate in environmental science and food safety. She is now a professor at George Washington University’s school of public health. A decade after we met, Kowalcyk played a leading role in campaigning for “test and hold,” a food-safety rule to prevent meat companies from shipping ground beef until it had tested negative for dangerous forms of E. coli. In 2013, the rule was finally adopted by the USDA. Few large E. coli outbreaks are today caused by ground beef. Eric Schlosser: Do we really want a food cartel? Similar test-and-hold rules could help eliminate Salmonella from poultry. A 2022 study by Consumer Reports found that almost one-third of the ground chicken sold at American supermarkets contained Salmonella—an extraordinarily high level. Strict food-safety regulation of the poultry industry in Denmark has reduced the level of Salmonella contamination in broiler chickens there from 14 to 0.3 percent—and, as a result, the number of people sickened by Salmonella has declined by 99.6 percent. But the Trump administration recently blocked the adoption of a new rule, about to go in effect after years of deliberation at the USDA, that would have prohibited the sale of poultry products highly contaminated with Salmonella, claiming the rule would have imposed “significant financial and operational burdens on American businesses and consumers.” The Meat Institute and the National Chicken Council backed the move. When it comes to food manufactured for infants, the federal government has enormous leverage that is going unused. The infant-formula industry is highly concentrated. Two companies, Abbott and Mead Johnson, control more than 80 percent of the market. Through the USDA’s Special Supplemental Nutrition Program for Women, Infants, and Children, or WIC, the federal government buys more than half of the infant formula consumed in the United States—meaning that it could swiftly mandate changes on behalf of infant health. But it hasn’t. The Republican-controlled Congress has refused to hold hearings on the botulism outbreaks or on legislation introduced in March that would expand pathogen testing of infant formula and strengthen congressional oversight. The idea of putting all federal food-safety efforts under one roof is a nonstarter. “We are paying all we care to pay”—that was the industry response in 1906, and that is the response today. It’s the rest of us who bear the cost.
The Atlantic
Creating the Perfect Female Monster
2026-09-03

Creating the Perfect Female Monster

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Subscribe here: Apple Podcasts | Spotify | YouTube | Overcast | Pocket Casts The breakout crime drama Furious opens with a scene that’s hard to watch: Catherine, a serial killer played by Lola Petticrew, is keeping a man prisoner in a row house and taunting him as he dies: “I don’t like the sounds you’re making.” The show’s creator, Liz Meriwether, is announcing right away that you will be spending time with a monster, and the discomfort amps up from there. As the show progresses, we learn that Catherine has been trafficked, and the men she is killing were involved in her abuse. In an era when “Epstein Island” exists, Catherine represents a specific kind of psychopathic serial killer. She is, as the title suggests, one of the Furies exacting revenge in a world that hasn’t managed to execute justice for her the legal way. Meriwether says she did not set out to make a statement on the state of female rage or American justice, or even a commentary on Epstein, yet the show may have hit a nerve because those issues are so alive right now. Nine years ago, women’s anger fueled the MeToo movement, and it seemed as if the way society confronts sexual harassment and abuse was on the brink of transformation. “Misogyny is more overt on the internet than it’s ever been,” the Atlantic staff writer Sophie Gilbert says. “And you think about what happened to that anger and what happened to that rage, and I know from my own perspective, you can’t live with that kind of anger for a decade. You just can’t. It has to burn out. It has to go somewhere, or you just get numb.” In this episode, Meriwether talks about the complications of humanizing a serial killer and how the project of creating complicated women characters has evolved in her decade of writing for television. And Gilbert talks about what it means that more women creators are owning the stories told about women. The following is a transcript of the episode: Hanna Rosin: A female monster is a rare but recurring creature. She often emerges at moments in history when women in society are collectively angry at the powers that be but unable to express it. Maybe they’re too defeated or maybe numb, so the monster gets revenge for them. Right now, there is a particularly interesting monster out there taking revenge on a fictional Jeffrey Epstein. It’s playing out on the Hulu show Furious—the season finale aired this week. Sophie Gilbert:  Well, it was one of those shows that became a slow burn, I think. Rosin: This is staff writer Sophie Gilbert. Gilbert:  When it first debuted, there wasn’t that much chatter about it. But then you started to see people watch, and it became this word-of-mouth hit. Rosin: Furious is a dark and sometimes disturbing TV show about a female serial killer, played by Lola Petticrew, who is pursued by a female FBI agent, played by Emmy Rossum. Gilbert:  So there’s a lot about it that is quite kind of tropey, old-school procedural. It’s a crime show. The cop and the killer being pursued, they have that kind of, Oh, we’re not so different, you and I, kind of thing going on. But at the same time, it was also like no other procedural I’ve ever seen, in the sense that in so many crime shows, you see women’s bodies used like window dressing almost. And this is a show that really seems to be made about women, not for women necessarily, but it’s interested in women. It’s interested in what happens to women when they go through traumatic experiences, what happens to women when they become angry. There’s so much that is kind of really deep and tied to these mythological elements of transformation and violence. And it just works well on so many levels. Rosin: The show’s mythological elements are in the name. It’s called Furious after the Furies, the Greek goddesses of vengeance. In the show, the Furies are implied to be serial killer Catherine, FBI agent Alice, and Alice’s mentor, Nora—three women who, in different ways, have all failed to find the justice they need. Gilbert:  So Nora Washington is the head of the FBI’s sex-crimes unit in the show. She’s played by Quincy Tyler Bernstine in this extraordinary performance. She’s so good. And she has a few speeches where she’s talking to Alice Black, sort of the main character in the show, who’s played by Emmy Rossum, who is trying to become an FBI agent under her wing. And she’s explaining to her the toll that this job takes, of looking into these horrendous sex crimes, these crimes often against underage girls, against children. You take on this responsibility when you do this job of doing this thing that no one else will do, to try and achieve a measure of justice, or certainly a measure of justice that is often impossible, and it changes you. Quincy Tyler Bernstine (as Nora Washington in Furious): Everyone likes it when there’s a sweet little woman lying on the floor, naked and cut up in pieces. I get so fucking angry. Gilbert: Because you have to become, in some ways, a monster to do this day after day. Bernstine: It doesn’t matter, anything you do. The ones who are really to blame don’t get caught. Never. Never. Gilbert: So there are all these sort of elemental themes of Greek tragedy and Greek drama that seem to come through. Bernstine: You think you’re ever gonna catch anyone who matters? Gilbert:  So this show starts with a scene of a woman whose name we later find out is Catherine—she’s played by Lola Petticrew—who is keeping a man prisoner in a Brooklyn row house on Halloween. Gilbert: And Catherine is wearing a mask, and she is eating candy corn, and she is clearly torturing this man. And then eventually, we see her murder him. Lola Petticrew (as Catherine Grace in Furious): I don’t really like listening to you. I don’t like the sounds you’re making. Gilbert: And the show begins in this really stark way of saying, This is who this character is. Like, Be horrified by this; be appalled by her actions. It’s sort of really uncomfortable to watch in lots of ways, really visceral. And then as the show goes on, you understand more about who she is. You understand her history. You understand why she’s doing what she’s doing, her flawed but in some ways very sympathetic logic. And you see her full humanity. And she becomes, yes, a serial killer, but one who does seem to be doing it for reasons that are, if not fully justifiable, you start to sort of empathize with them a little bit more. Rosin: Yeah. And here’s the difficult thing: The parallels to the current world are apparent. This is essentially a Jeffrey Epstein setup. It’s a setup about people who are portrayed as rich. There’s a man at the center of it. There’s a sort of a twisted luring in of teenagers and young women. It really has obvious parallels to the world we are currently living in. And here’s a woman exacting justice because the justice system wouldn’t. Rosin: So as I describe it, it is sympathetic. You are, in some ways, supposed to root for this person who at the very first scene is gruesome. Gilbert:  I don’t know if you’re supposed to root for her, but it becomes impossible not to, in some ways. Gilbert: And I think that’s part of the trick. The show is not black and white morally. It reveals things, and it explains them, and it offers context for them. And we, as human beings, watch it, and we have our own context, and we have our own life experiences, and we add. And we’re supposed to make our own judgments, but I don’t think the show is telling us what to sort of presume one way or another. And that, to me, is what makes it so interesting as an experience. But yes, this character Jay Easton is, obviously—he’s certainly in the Jeffrey Epstein mold. He’s a billionaire. He’s revealed to have preyed on hundreds, maybe thousands of young, underage women. And yet, what again is striking about the show is that he’s not really interesting. He’s not a character who the show is sort of interested in delving into, really. The show is much more curious about the impact he’s had on the lives of women and girls, and that, I think, is often what goes underplayed or what is missing in our Epstein discourse as well. So it’s shining a light on a different facet of this very topical issue. Rosin: As I watched episode after episode, I had a very uncomfortable reaction that I’m guessing other people had, that the creator of the show, Liz Meriwether—who we’ll talk to later—says she intended: I was not, not rooting for the serial killer, this deeply unlikeable, twisted, dark, criminal young woman who was effectively hunting down the men who had abused her when she was a teenager. Gilbert:  Without being, like, morally endorsing murder, it can be cathartic to see where they go with it and to imagine what happens when there’s no justice possible. What do you do with all that rage? Rosin: Sophie wrote about the show recently for The Atlantic, about how the grief, anger, and rage in Furious reminded her of a different time, nearly a decade ago, when similar feelings of anger generated headlines, spawned think pieces and hashtags, and started the MeToo movement that ultimately fizzled out. Rosin:  Yeah. So what does that reflect in the culture then? A sense of frustration, helplessness? What does that mean? Gilbert:  For me, the show really resonated when I was thinking about—first of all, it’s been almost a decade since the MeToo moment, which is just wild to think about anyway, all this time. But when you think about what happened at the end of 2017, the revelations about Harvey Weinstein, the revelations about all these men— Reporter (from ABC News): New Yorker magazine reporting new claims of sexual harassment, assault, and rape. One of his accusers going to police, secretly recording her confrontation with Weinstein, but no charges were ever filed. Gilbert: —there was so much in the sense that we were in this moment of societal transformation and women’s anger. There were so many books written about women’s anger. There were TV shows written about women’s anger. It seemed like rage and fury, and righteous fury, was this great theme in our culture that might have a kind of revolutionary force, that it might help upend systems and overturn injustice. Reporter (from CBS Miami): Instead of naming one person, Time magazine named its 2017 Person of the Year the “silence breakers,” the name given to the women who came forward to report sexual harassment and assault, sparking the MeToo movement. Gilbert: And then you look nine years later and you think, Wow, actually, none of that has happened. We’ve, if anything, lost rights. Misogyny is more overt on the internet than it’s ever been. More and more injustices and horrific crimes against women and girls are revealed. I mean, really, it feels almost daily. We have an overtly misogynist president, I would say. And you think about what happened to that anger and what happened to that rage, and I know from my own perspective, you can’t live with that kind of anger for a decade. You just can’t. It has to burn out. It has to go somewhere, or you just get numb, I think. And so what this show is doing, which really connected with my own sense of just being unable to feel that rage day after day after day, is wondering what happens when it becomes something else, when it takes a different direction, when it realizes that justice is impossible, and so vengeance is the next best thing. I wanna ask about the broad sweep of things. In your book, Girl on Girl, the thesis is that pop culture taught Millennial women to hate themselves, or maybe just that pop culture of the ’90s and aughts hated women in a certain way. This is a show conceived of by women, written by women, in which a woman and the Furies do get revenge. Does that at least agency, even if it’s a perverse agency, does that represent something different, like controlling the narrative? Gilbert:  Yes, the stories that we get now about women, about the complexities of their lives, the conditions that they’re living under, like the truth of their experience, are made by women about women. There’s so much that is so much better than it was even 10 years ago. And I was reading an interview with Liz Meriwether, the creator of Furious, where she said—because she’s the creator of a much-beloved sitcom, one of my favorites, called New Girl—and she was mentioning the notes that she got from Fox in terms of sort of making the character more likable and making it broadly more appealing to men, and how now, making this show, Furious, there was none of that. There’s much more appetite I think, or at least more understanding on the part of people commissioning work like this, that you can be interested in women as a subject and that will make great art that will connect with viewers and be enough of a justification. We don’t need to always be kind of shushing women in a way. Rosin:  Yeah. And it’s not just, you can be interested in women. You can be interested—I mean, the task she’s setting out for a viewer here is: You can be interested in a woman who’s, like you said, a monster. From the very first scene, she’s the exact opposite of a beautiful dead victim. She couldn’t be less likable, at least in the very beginning. Gilbert:  Yes. And the show really trusts us as audiences to see that and to keep watching, which I think is fascinating all on its own. Like, there’s this idea that we don’t need to be coddled into finding this woman cute and sexy.  (Laughs.) Gilbert: We can see what she is and we can be hooked anyway, because we’re sophisticated enough consumers of media to understand that the payoff might be worth it. Rosin: After the break, we talk to the Furious showrunner, Liz Meriwether, about why she created a monster you don’t necessarily hate. Rosin: Liz Merriweather started as a comedy writer. As Sophie mentioned, her breakout hit was New Girl. Then she wrote The Dropout, a show about Theranos founder and convicted fraudster Elizabeth Holmes—also not a classic lovable type. And then Dying for Sex, about a woman who has a terminal-cancer diagnosis—so potentially a perfect victim—but instead, she decides to spend her final months having a lot of sex. We’ve talked a lot on this episode about post-MeToo progress being stalled. But this—this creation, by female writers, of female characters who test the bounds of likeability—is also a kind of progress. I talk to Merriweather—who is in the writers’ room working on Season 2 of Furious—about how her ability to write female characters exactly how she wants them has evolved. So the terrible word that’s imposed on female characters forever, which is likable— female politicians, everybody, likability—from the very first scene, you make the decision to make Catherine not likable. Did you get any pushback on that? Like, Put some woman in there who is more pure, or a sort of more recognizable person you can attach yourself to? Meriwether: As somebody who’s been doing this for about 15 years now, and when I first started doing it, got that note all the time, I was surprised that I didn’t get that note. Rosin: Can you actually talk about that? ’Cause you were mostly known as a comedy writer. New Girl was the breakout hit, and when you were writing that, what were the conversations like about how weird or unconventional a main character who’s a woman could be? Meriwether: Yeah. The notes I got were, like—the first pilot I wrote was called Sluts. It was kind of Girls before Girls, in a way. It was three girls in New York who just, like, had a lot of sex and just were deciding to get their lives together and sort of stop having so much sex. And this was for Fox. It was never gonna get on the air. But that was the first pilot that I wrote, and I had conversations with executives that were like, She either is smart, or she likes sex, and that was a binary thing that I had to genuinely have a conversation with somebody about. Where I was like, No, you can be smart and also have had a lot of sex. Rosin: So you were in your 20s when you wrote that pilot, when you wrote Sluts. Meriwether: Yeah, I was, like, 25 or something. Yeah. Rosin: And did you already know, I’m trying to push against some kind of tropes or ideas of how female characters should be? Meriwether: Yeah. I mean, I think I’ve always come from a place of, like, I’m gonna make jokes about it or try to come at it from a little bit of a sneakier place. I’ve never been a writer that’s been very I have a big statement I wanna make. Which is, honestly, why this show kinda scared me a little bit, because I was like, I don’t get to hide as much. I don’t get to kind of, like, you know, tap-dance and make jokes and say, like, No, look over here. Look over here. Look over here. But yes, I’ve always been interested in trying to put women on screen that are not what you have seen before. That’s been something that I’ve been interested in for a long time. I just feel like we’ve taken steps forward and steps back over the past 15 years, but there’s definitely more complicated female characters on television now than there was back then. When New Girl first aired, I did a lot of press. It was, like, me and Whitney Cummings had comedies coming out, and [people] were like, We can’t believe there are, like, comedies written by women—more than one comedy written by women. That was like the main thrust of the press. And that would never be a story nowadays. So yes, there, I mean, there is growth. I think that there’s also a lot of moving forward then moving backwards. Rosin: Yeah. For this episode, we also interviewed my Atlantic colleague Sophie Gilbert, who wrote this book Girl on Girl, which is about how pop culture of the late ’90s and aughts hated women or taught Millennial women to hate themselves. And I was thinking about you because you, that’s your era of pop culture. I also saw that you signed on to write the biopic of Britney Spears, who is the ultimate example of how the late ’90s and early aughts chewed some women up. And so I wondered if that was part of what motivates you or what excites you about these kinds of projects. Meriwether: I think so. Yeah. I mean, I do kinda just keep my head down and keep moving forward. Like, I don’t go from a place of like I have an agenda. But yes, those are the characters that really excite me—the women where it’s, like, very hard to put into words how you feel about them. You don’t know if you’re rooting for them or not. I do think it’s a very interesting, fun place to be as a writer, maybe because of all of the notes I got about likability and the desire to sort of get away from that. But I also think it’s not interesting to make somebody completely evil. I think that middle space is really interesting. Rosin: And maybe it just feels more true. Meriwether: Yeah. There’s also a tendency to, say, victimize women in a different way, where it’s like they had no agency in what happened to them or, you know, they are completely products of a broken system, which I feel like is not fair either and a little bit patronizing and boring, honestly. So I think I’m interested in, like, all the nuances of why people do the things they do. Rosin: Sophie, you know what I was thinking about? The Olivia Rodrigo reprisal of Lilith Fair. Olivia Rodrigo: 45,000 people at this festival tonight. Can you believe it? Rosin: I don’t know if you saw the documentary about Lilith Fair. Rosin:  So I watched that documentary, and I think I might have cried, honestly, because I thought, Oh, well, that era is dead. Like, We will never, ever have that back again. [Sounds from the Lilith Fair: Building a Mystery trailer] Rosin: A sort of collection of women getting together, talking about their music, entirely made for and run by women, and yet here it is reprised this summer. And I thought, Oh, maybe the sense of agency that’s emerging, it does feel different than what you wrote about [in your book]. It does feel different than the Britney Spears era, the era in which women died by those cultural stories. Now it feels like they’re writing the stories, and so even though they might be grim or they might be inspirational, they are controlling the narrative. Gilbert:  That Lilith documentary was so good and I think so sad, for exactly the reasons that you said, which was: It seemed the last moment when this idea of a women-centric society, or at least an alternative society, seemed possible. (Laughs.) And then everything that happened afterwards in the 2000s, and just this real decade of victimizing women for living their lives in public. But yes, again, we have this moment of a mainstream pop star holding a massive music festival for abortion-rights charities and reproductive rights, which you can’t imagine someone like Taylor Swift doing that. Rodrigo: Today I’m so aware of all the women who came before me, who fought for me to enjoy the rights that I enjoy today. Gilbert: All right, it’s not as subversive and as edgy as what riot-grrrl musicians were doing in the ’90s, but it’s political. It’s active. It’s angry about the way that things are, and it’s willing to take a stand to change them. And then it also—I think the thing that I found so fascinating about this festival, Daisy Chain, was that it just demonstrates the power that women have in the music industry. Of all the different cultural industries, music really seems to be one where women are dominant right now—where they sell the most records, where they’re making the most interesting music, where they’re the ones that we are drawn to. They have these huge tours, and they have this huge fandom, and they are front and center in music and powerful in a way that men are not. And that, I think, is interesting right now, in this moment. And the question of why, I don’t know. But if we’re looking for things to be hopeful about, that’s definitely one. Rosin:  Right, ’cause in thinking about women’s anger, I got stuck in a kind of limited narrative, which is, Oh, in 2017, women were able to channel the anger in a productive direction. People told their stories. Sort of a dam burst open. There were truths out there, and that was cathartic. And in 2026, we get a serial killer. So then I was trying to think, Well, maybe that’s not that simple. There are places that anger finds an out that are more joyful or productive, and so this is one that I look to. Gilbert:  Yeah. Olivia Rodrigo just released a song called “Serena Joy,” which is named, obviously, after the character in The Handmaid’s Tale who has helped subjugate other women.  One of the lyrics in it is, “If you can’t throw a punch, you can learn how to spit.” Gilbert: Just, it’s this idea that numbness is not inevitable. We don’t have to just be complacent forever and ever and ever while our rights are eroded and things are taken away—that in some ways, cultural power is still there, and that there are still women sort of taking a stand for things, and in some ways, storytelling is able to do such interesting work now because we are in a different place than we were in 2017, and we’re much more open to ideas about what’s possible, even if it doesn’t seem like it’s easily achievable. Rosin:  Yeah. Well, Sophie, thank you so much for talking this through with me today. Gilbert:  Oh, it’s a pleasure. Thank you for having me. How ‘Somatics’ Became the Solution to Modern Life
2026-09-03

How ‘Somatics’ Became the Solution to Modern Life

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
When clients go to Sybil Ottenstein for therapy, they tend to arrive with answers. Many of them have seen shrinks before. They can tell her which events from their past were formative, and point to the ripple effects in their current life. They’re like detectives, she told me, with all the dots connected on their corkboard of evidence. Intellectually, they’ve cracked the case. The problem is that they don’t feel any better. That’s why they’re seeking something different from Ottenstein: somatic psychotherapy. Traditional talk therapy could be seen as a top-down approach. You identify problems in your thinking, your habits, or your life, and hope that by working through them, you can resolve physical manifestations of emotional issues—the chest palpitations of anxiety, the stomachache of stress. Somatic therapy, by contrast, works from the bottom up. Ottenstein typically starts her sessions by asking clients to slow down and pay attention to corporeal sensations: “What does it feel like in your chest? In your throat?” As they talk, she encourages them to stay in their body, to let themselves feel whatever is coming up. People cry. They yawn. They scream. They stretch. They dance. The word somatic, according to Merriam-Webster, merely means “of, relating to, or affecting the body.” But recently, it’s been popping up everywhere, appended to a variety of practices that involve tuning in to physical symptoms in order to ameliorate psychological ones. Somatic therapists like Ottenstein have noticed a significant bump in interest; one of the major somatic-therapy training programs reported that its applicants more than doubled from 2020 to 2023. Other practitioners offer somatic dance, somatic yoga, somatic workouts. (The terms seem a touch redundant.) Read: How did healing ourselves get so exhausting? On Instagram, hundreds of thousands of posts appear with the hashtag #somatic. A “somatic exercise” trainer who goes by the “Workout Witch” (nearly 2 million followers) teaches small, repetitive movements meant to discharge negative feelings—curl your head down for “sorrow/despair,” pull your right ear for “overwhelm”—and promises that “trauma can be released from your hips.” All over my feed, fit young women are humming, hitting their sternum, pinching their nose bridge, and bouncing their knees all the way to inner peace. Read: These Millennials can’t get out of their head Much of the time, as with many other wellness ventures, someone stands to make money. At one somatic-workout class I attended, where a staffer told me that the goal was to “move stagnant energy out,” the lobby featured a gift-shop corner with jewelry, T-shirts, and $65 “body gloss” from a brand called Oui the People. But clearly, plenty of Americans are willing to pay for the promise of a psychic cleanse. Practitioners refer to “stuck energy,” to “trapped emotions,” to “resetting” a nervous system caught in fight-or-flight mode. “We have all these ways of just storing that gunk inside,” Ottenstein told me. I pictured, uncomfortably, a thick, black goo running through my veins. Somatics—the whole roomy umbrella of practices—might seem like an odd fit for the information age, when people are more accustomed to finding answers on the internet than within themselves. But that could be precisely the draw for thinkers and analyzers who are tired of ruminating in anxious circles, who spend their days in front of a laptop and nights scrolling an iPhone. For millennia, people have yearned to break out of the confines of their bodies; they’ve searched—through religion, spirituality, drugs—for transcendence. Now, for some, the existential quest is inverted: Instead of seeking to escape their prison of flesh and bones, they want to reinhabit it. The origin of somatics is difficult to pinpoint, though it’s certainly not recent. Some credit Wilhelm Reich, a student of Freud’s, who theorized in the early 1900s that patients held tension in their bodies—“muscular armor”—as a subconscious defense against trauma, leading to aches, pains, and inflammation. But it wasn’t until the 1970s that the field began to formalize, developed in part by the American philosophy professor Thomas Hanna, who defined somatics as the study of “the body as perceived from within.” That same decade, a UC Berkeley biophysics doctoral student, Peter Levine, developed “somatic experiencing”: a therapeutic method based on the idea that people, like other animals, react to trauma with a flight, fight, or freeze response, powered by a burst of adrenaline. After the source of danger is gone, Levine argued, they have pent-up “survival energy” to unleash: They need to physically shake it off. He pointed to videos of impalas, quaking and buckling after surviving a cheetah attack. When humans go through something upsetting, Ottenstein told me, “we go to work the next day.” This idea—that trauma is held in the body, and thus must be physically remedied—was once fairly niche. In recent years, though, it has been jolted into prominence. The psychiatrist Bessel van der Kolk’s book The Body Keeps the Score, which argues for somatic treatments of PTSD, was first released in 2014 and was not an immediate hit. In 2021, it reached the No. 1 spot on the New York Times best-seller list for paperback nonfiction. And although it has been accused of resting on shaky science, it has continued to dominate the charts for years. Van der Kolk wrote about violent assaults, wartime atrocities, severe childhood abuse—circumstances that many readers hadn’t directly experienced. Yet The Body Keeps the Score seemed to resonate anyway. When it gained momentum, lots of Americans had spent the previous year safe in their homes, baking bread and taking Zoom calls. (“When people say the pandemic has been a collective trauma,” van der Kolk told The Atlantic that year, “I say, absolutely not.”) Most had also, however, been detached from the rich sensory experience of interacting with the external world. Now here was a book about people who’d felt estranged from their own body—and then, through simple movement, play, and touch, found their way back to their surroundings, and to themselves. Read: The self-help that no one needs right now In the years since, most people have emerged from pandemic isolation. Yet the share of Americans who say they frequently experience stress, which has been rising for decades, has continued its upward trend. Talk therapy remains popular, but it’s not always affordable or accessible—and much of the time, it doesn’t take physical symptoms into account. Meanwhile, many medical doctors don’t consider psychological causes of physical ailments. Stress in particular is “like this mythical creature,” Aditi Nerurkar, a physician at Harvard Medical School, told me. “And we don’t have a lot of time. We have to focus on your hemoglobin. Did you get a mammogram? Did you get a pap smear? What is your cholesterol?” No wonder, then, that somatics is a flourishing industry. Many Americans already distrust medical institutions; maybe they want to bypass the dismissive doctor, or the therapist who wants to rehash every daddy issue but can’t seem to fix anything. So they turn to this strange constellation of New Age–y courses, TikTok sages, and licensed counselors. Some offerings target people with capital-T Trauma; others claim to be for just about anyone. Many present themselves as alternatives to more traditional paths to mental well-being. The question is whether they can really help the frazzled masses. Somatics does get a lot of things right. Plenty of bodily sensations are, at root, psychological. Some communication does need to happen between the brain and the rest of the body. That’s how you know to respond to danger: The amygdala triggers the nervous system to increase your heart rate, deliver more blood to muscles, and release a flood of adrenaline. When I spoke with Nerurkar, she rattled off an incomplete list of physical symptoms that might result from stress: headaches, dizziness, nausea, vision changes. When she was in residency training, she said, she experienced what felt like a “stampede of wild horses” across her chest and was convinced that she had a heart problem—until her doctors, having conducted a battery of tests, recommended that she seriously consider chilling out. And many components of somatic practice have evidence to back them up. Interoception, or awareness of one’s own physical signals, really can be important for well-being—perhaps because it increases people’s sense of attunement with their body, connection with the present moment, or agency over otherwise vague and inscrutable sensations. Mindfulness meditation, which involves interoception, has been shown to decrease stress and anxiety and even reduce physical pain. Yoga and breathing exercises can decrease amygdala activation and lead to better concentration, memory, and executive function. But the somatics packaging of these techniques is alluring partly because of the emotional liberation it promises. Somaticists tend to describe a clear path: identify a physical signal, connect it to its psychic cause, then let it go. If the nervous system overactivates, surely you can just reset it. If the body holds trauma, surely the body can relinquish it. These were always just metaphors, though, and imperfect ones. The amygdala doesn’t have a reboot button. There is no viscous gunk of emotional pain to flush out. The Body Keeps the Score has been somewhat debunked; critics have argued that van der Kolk incorrectly described certain studies, and that he seems to have made up others entirely. (Responding to the writer Emi Nietfeld’s critique in Mother Jones, van der Kolk said that at times he drew on unpublished data.) Trauma experts have pushed back on his wholesale condemnation of talk therapy, which can be, according to a mountain of evidence, very helpful for treating PTSD. There’s also something ironic in how certain somaticists—ostensibly advocates for the mind-body connection—talk as if the two are separated by a nearly unbridgeable gap: the physical burden of trauma unreachable through thinking or conversation, and perhaps not even accessible to conscious awareness. Read: Why we’re still talking about the ‘trauma plot’ So somatics isn’t a miracle cure. And it’s probably right to be skeptical of influencers who seek to collect on people’s need to feel at home in their body. But that doesn’t mean somatics can’t be helpful. Much of it, after all, turns out to be pretty straightforward. Movement is good, generally. Breathing is good. Stepping away from your screen—stretching your limbs, remembering you are real and tangible and mortal—is good. When I signed up for that somatic-exercise class, I expected that I might leave rolling my eyes , but I secretly hoped I’d walk out lighter, more alive and connected to myself and the world. What I found was something in between. The actual workout couldn’t have been more basic: It consisted largely of squats, jumping jacks, crunches, some yoga—downward dog, child’s pose. Occasionally the instructor would remind us to check in with our bodies: “What are you being invited into? And I don’t mean the burpee.” Or: “Let the chin enter the conversation.” Otherwise it hardly seemed uniquely somatic, or uniquely anything. Then again, I had the post-workout endorphin glow. I’d left my apartment and shut my phone in a locker. An hour later, I exited the studio somewhat baffled. But walking out onto a busy Manhattan street, I felt a little more conscious of the sun on my cheek, the solidness of the ground pushing against my feet. This was silly, I thought. And also: Maybe I’ll come back. ​​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic

An Inflection Point for Prediction Markets
2026-09-02

An Inflection Point for Prediction Markets

The Atlantic | 2026-09-02

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. The day before Donald Trump’s second State of the Union address, the former Representative George Santos shared a video of himself saying, in no uncertain terms, that he was “going to be there.” The next day, he didn’t show up. According to the prediction-market company Kalshi, that was the plan—the firm alleged that he made roughly $17,000 by betting on the platform that he wouldn’t appear at the speech. Kalshi flagged the wager to federal authorities, and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), the agency that regulates prediction markets, fined Santos over it in July. On Monday, Kalshi doubled down, levying a fine of its own and giving him the platform’s first-ever lifetime ban. Prediction-market companies, which let users make bets on the outcomes of all sorts of real-world events, have fully entered the mainstream over the past few years. You can find their logos at major sporting events, on cable-news broadcasts, and at awards shows. They’re easy to access and, thanks to a recent advertising blitz, essentially ubiquitous. Trading volumes on Kalshi and Polymarket, the two market leaders, have exceeded $200 billion and $100 billion this year, respectively. But these companies have strenuously resisted the idea that they should be thought of as gambling platforms. “I just don’t really know what this has to do with gambling,” Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour said of his company last year. (By avoiding this classification, prediction markets have also been able to operate in states where sports betting is banned.) Instead, Kalshi and Polymarket have suggested that their platforms are digital oracles: potent indicators of what the future might hold, fueled by the wisdom of the crowd. In recent months, a nascent crackdown on insider trading has been exposing a fundamental tension in these companies’ guiding principles. If the goal is to create fair markets and not get targeted by the government, then they must enforce a ban on insider trading, as U.S. regulators already do. But if the goal is forecasting—aligning these markets’ odds with what’s really going to happen in the world—then allowing bettors to trade on inside information could actually benefit these platforms. This, at least, was once the thinking of Polymarket CEO Shayne Coplan, who described his platform last year as “the most accurate thing we have as mankind right now, until someone else creates some sort of a super crystal ball.” In response to a question about his approach to preventing insider trading, he said in November that Polymarket “creates this financial incentive for people to go and divulge the information to the market”—theoretically creating more accurate projections. This may help explain why Polymarket has taken so long to clamp down on insider trading. The company didn’t explicitly ban the practice until March, nearly six years after its founding. Kalshi took a different tack: When I reached out to the company in January, its representatives stressed that insider trading was unambiguously prohibited. (But the question of which offenders are penalized—and what those punishments look like—still seems to be decided on a case-by-case basis.) This year, both companies have been ramping up their enforcement efforts. The shift began back in January, when The Wall Street Journal reported that, in the lead-up to the U.S. capture of former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro, an unknown trader had made more than $400,000 betting that Maduro would soon be out of power. To many observers, it certainly looked like insider trading—and lawmakers from both parties began pushing for stricter regulation. A few months later, Polymarket notified the Department of Justice that one of the soldiers involved in the operation had allegedly placed the wager, and the agency indicted him for trading on nonpublic information (he has pleaded not guilty). According to a spokesperson for Polymarket, the company’s insider-trading investigations have led to “nearly 100 referrals to law enforcement,” and its work on the Maduro bets and on a separate case involving a Google employee resulted in the “first and second prediction markets insider trading cases charged in the United States.” This week, in addition to the Santos ban, Kalshi announced several new settlements with public figures accused of insider trading on its platform, including a North Carolinian candidate for the House who has admitted to betting on her own race. (“I bet on myself,” she wrote in an emailed statement. “It was a dumb mistake, and as soon as I learned there was an issue, I worked to make it right.”) And this past weekend, Trump’s longtime teleprompter operator paid $172,000 to settle the CFTC’s claims that he was betting on the individual words that the president would use in his speeches—claims first made by Kalshi. Regulators don’t have a unified approach to this sector. As The New York Times points out, 20 states are now waging legal battles over whether these companies should be subject to the same laws as online sportsbooks. In an apparent defense of the industry, the CFTC has sued some of the states suing prediction-market companies for infringing on federal jurisdiction. Polymarket’s international platform, which runs on the blockchain, is off-limits to U.S. traders and isn’t bound by the CFTC’s rules on insider trading. But even as major legal questions remain unresolved, what’s indisputable is that insider trading is a red line for U.S regulators. Polymarket’s and Kalshi’s stricter enforcement of those rules may help keep them in the federal government’s good graces, and the appearance of fairness may also prevent people from betting elsewhere. But in taking these steps, these businesses will have to reckon with how they present themselves. They can cling to the idea that they’re not gambling platforms—but regulators may not always see it that way. The summer started with a diarrhea-causing parasite called Cyclospora cayetanensis, which infested iceberg lettuce. Then came news of Shiga toxin–producing bacteria in organic frozen berries, and a Salmonella outbreak linked to Whole Foods guacamole that eventually got traced to tainted jalapeños. (Pico de gallo, salsa fresca, and a variety of spicy dips have also been implicated.) Ricotta-style cheese was recalled for Listeria. Both Salmonella and E. coli were found in products from the aptly named Everything Sprouts. And 10,000 cases of lightly fried tofu were flagged for potential contamination with printing ink. The Atlantic
The Met’s Other Failure
2026-09-02

The Met’s Other Failure

The Atlantic | 2026-09-02

Visit Original Website
The Metropolitan Museum of Art’s announcement this week that it is canceling next year’s exhibition on the fashion of John Galliano perhaps shouldn’t have come as a surprise. From the start, Galliano was a controversial pick for the museum’s Costume Institute, which launches its new show each year with the splashy Met Gala. In 2011, he lost his job as creative director of Dior after a video surfaced of him making a drunken anti-Semitic rant; later that year, a French court found him guilty of the crime of making anti-Semitic and anti-Asian insults. The New York Times also recently described claims that Galliano had made anti-Semitic comments during an outburst in 2019. (Through a spokesperson, Galliano denied the 2019 episode.) But not long after his Dior exit, Galliano—who has always traded on his bad-boy image while producing clothes of astounding beauty and theatricality—was welcomed back into the industry. After so publicly hitting rock-bottom, the designer, who said addiction to drugs and alcohol led to the rants, apologized and went to rehab. Maison Margiela then hired him. Celebrities such as Zendaya wore his designs; this year, the fast-fashion retailer Zara announced a partnership with him to great excitement. Anna Wintour, Vogue’s global editorial director and the Met Gala’s chair, also happens to be his longtime friend and champion. In the process of planning the Met show, Galliano, Wintour, and Andrew Bolton, the Costume Institute’s curator, consulted with Jewish leaders, and the exhibition itself intended to—as the museum initially announced in July—explore how the designer’s “derogatory conduct” had “fundamentally altered his career and public reception.” Those efforts did little to assuage wider public opinion. Museum donors threatened to pull funding; the speaker of the New York city council called the choice of Galliano a “serious mistake.” On Monday, the museum released a statement saying that the show “will not proceed as planned.” It was, Wintour said in the statement, “the right decision.” It is also a blow to the museum and its reputation that could have been avoided. For most of the Costume Institute’s 80-year history, it has followed an unofficial rule: Don’t do a solo show about a living designer—because such an exhibition could be seen as a form of advertising instead of scholarship, especially if the designer has curatorial input. Although such shows are now commonplace, the Met’s fashion wing has always prided itself on “setting the standard” for conservation and collection, and has devoted only two exhibitions to living designers: Yves Saint Laurent in 1983 and Rei Kawakubo in 2017. Both were controversial exceptions to long-standing norms intended to separate cultural analysis from commercialism. Putting on a museum exhibition of Galliano’s work, even while interrogating his past behavior, was always going to be ethically dicey because the designer still stands to benefit from the publicity and prestige that the show bestows. This saga suggests that the Costume Institute, and perhaps all fashion museums, should forgo retrospectives of living artists—and revisit why rules discouraging them were necessary to begin with. Museums have been routinely collecting and displaying clothes as a branch of the decorative arts since the 19th century, but early fashion exhibits typically featured only historical dress. Contemporary fashion was considered insufficiently artistic, and highlighting it was seen as overly promotional. But as fashion designers became household names in the 1960s, more contemporary pieces found their way into thematic exhibitions. Usually, the items were in shows featuring the work of several designers, including at the Met, which minimized the appearance of endorsing a particular label. The Costume Institute, which opened to the public in 1946, didn’t devote an entire show to an individual living designer until 1983, when Diana Vreeland—a former Vogue editor in chief—curated a retrospective of Yves Saint Laurent’s work in honor of his label’s 25th anniversary. Saint Laurent was involved in the exhibition and attended the opening fundraiser, both of which were underwritten by the firm that had long designed his textiles. The decision was contentious. Critics argued that it canonized a designer whose work had not yet stood the test of time, while blurring the boundary between art and advertising. The general public, though, was clearly captivated: The show drew more than 125,000 visitors in just its first month at a time when the average Met exhibition drew 500,000 visitors a year, Women’s Wear Daily reported. Despite its popularity, the experiment was not repeated under the next curator, or the next. Indeed, the Times later reported that the Costume Institute had adopted an “unofficial policy” of not doing monographic shows on living designers due to the “charges of commercialism” that the exhibition drew. Designers, however, took note of the public’s growing interest in fashion heritage and realized that their own histories could be a valuable source of design inspiration and cultural capital. In 1987, Dior created an archives department; most major fashion labels followed. Fashion-industry archivists started competing with museum curators to buy back their own vintage pieces at auction. Some fashion houses founded their own museums. Others turned their archives into touring exhibitions, large on spectacle and light on scholarship. By no coincidence, over the course of the 1990s, fashion studies gained traction as an academic discipline. “Suddenly, fashion was about identity, the body, gender, power, and visual representation,” Valerie Steele, a fashion historian and curator, wrote in Reinvention and Restlessness, a book created for an exhibition at the Museum at FIT. Fashion not only explained the past, the thinking went, but also expressed the ambitions and anxieties of the present. But the mingling of intellectual analysis and business interests hasn’t occurred without debate: Privately owned objects aren’t subject to the same strict conservation requirements as museum artifacts. And as museums started borrowing archival pieces directly from fashion houses, sometimes a designer’s “cooperation” with an exhibition stretched to input or approval—another ethical gray area that can compromise curatorial objectivity and authority. The ethics of fashion exhibitions got even more questionable in 2000, when the Guggenheim mounted a show about the Italian designer Giorgio Armani. The museum, which does not collect fashion, received a $15 million donation from the designer; the move was widely viewed as a transparent quid pro quo. “It’s all very well for an art gallery to declare the equality of ‘high’ and ‘low’ art forms, and to enjoy the boost in attendance that may bring,” one critic wrote at the time, “but the challenges and risks of shows like this can outweigh any benefits.” Whether they’re focused on pants or paintings, museum exhibitions don’t just publicize and promote the work of living artists; their imprimatur actually raises its market value. The Armani show had the distinction of prompting a museum-industry trade group and the IRS to update their ethical guidelines on corporate sponsorship to encourage greater transparency. Still, shows about living designers—produced with the help of those designers—became more common, and eventually they lost much of their stigma. For many years, in fact, the Met was one of the few holdouts. Its policy changed only when Bolton became curator in charge in 2016 and announced a show on the Japanese designer Rei Kawakubo. Bolton, according to a Times report, explicitly “wanted to challenge the idea” that museums couldn’t do monographs on living designers, and wanted to “demonstrate that ‘fashion is a living art.’” Kawakubo’s involvement was crucial to the museum’s show; every piece came from her archives, and that year, she served as the honorary chair for the Met Gala. Galliano, with his past behavior, complicates the ethics equation even more. Museums, of course, regularly exhibit works by artists with unsavory pasts—but, crucially, those artists are usually dead. They can no longer personally profit or walk the red carpet on opening night. And unlike fine art, fashion is built in to everyday consumerism. Few people can afford to leave a museum and buy a Basquiat. Many more can pop over to Zara for a piece from Galliano’s forthcoming collection. The art of fashion may be too enmeshed with the business of fashion for the two to ever be completely untangled. But museums can easily forestall self-inflicted scandals such as the one at the Met by not putting living designers on precarious pedestals. In the announcement of the show’s cancellation, Wintour called the move “John’s decision”; many news outlets subsequently reported that the designer “bowed out” or “withdrew” from the exhibition. This framing casts the news as Galliano’s noble self-sacrifice, and distances Wintour and the Met from the whole sorry affair. It also inadvertently highlights the conflict of interest central to this kind of show. An ethical, independent museum exhibition could have gone on without its subject’s participation. Or, better yet, it would not have sought its subject’s input in the first place.

So being opposed to ethnic cleansing is a 'leftist' purity-cult thing? The quandary one faces when the creator of something one likes demonstrates unpalatable values is an interesting one. Some would suggest that the creation (whether it be a Linux distribution, a novel, or a body of music) can be separated from the artist. If you can do this mental separation, then you can appreciate the creation, and not let it be sullied by the faults of the creator. This is the (slighly forced) logic of the 1Password CEO. Of course if your values align with those of the creator, then there is no quandary at all. For many though, this separation is difficult - particularly with art. We think that we can appreciate art on its own merits, but our appreciation is often bundled with the image we have of the artist. Our appreciation of the music of the Rolling Stones, for example is tied up with their romanticised image of being outlaw pirate bohemians. We semi-conciously attach this context to the music, and develop and appreciation in which the sound of the music itself is only a part. So if we grow up in the 1970s when English glam rock was all the rage, and later learn that Gary Glitter was a pedophile, that bundle of music and context is tainted, and many, if not most, can no longer enjoy the Gary Glitter music they liked in their youth. In truth, artists and creaters are all flawed to some extent, in one way or another. In the case of DHH, it seemed to be the fact that he publicised his views on race and immigration in a blog post that caused the commotion. We didn't know it beforehand, and had he chosen to tone down his views, we might be none the wiser even now. I hadn't previously even heard of his Omarchy project, but because he has presented himself as a distasteful character (in my opinion), I am less likely to try it out. But what would I do if I was heavily invested in Ruby on Rails? Would I migrate onto some other platform? I imagine I might search around for excuses to avoid the time and expense that would incur. In practice, we seldom come up against absolute "red lines". Try to avoid support of people whose values are distasteful, but we balance this against the cost of doing so. If the disgust is great, we are willing to tolerate a higher cost. At first it seems a bit compromising, and spineless, but I don't think this sort of moral valuation is wrong - I think it is better than taking an extremist uncompromising stance on everything. CERN Transitioning From RHEL To Debian
2026-09-02

CERN Transitioning From RHEL To Debian

Slashdot | 2026-09-02

Visit Original Website
I'm a bit surprised they didn't go for Rocky Linux. Nothing wrong with Debian, mind, but Rocky is downstream from CentOS/RHEL and would be an easier migration. Debian even shipped 32-bit x86 kernels until Debian 12, so it supports architectures longer than other large Linux distributions.A major factor I'm guessing. Debian even shipped 32-bit x86 kernels until Debian 12, so it supports architectures longer than other large Linux distributions. RHEL to Debian. Clearly they want to use a major distribution. Not something that may not be around next year. Roky would probably have the same problem that made them switch. CERN will have a lot of radiation hardened and the like hardware that would be hard and expensive to replace. Based on the skim of their forum, they declined to support citing their desire for 1:1 binary compatibility with RHEL. AlmaLinux considered it, but the compilation costs (which also pull in EPEL) are very high. Then there is the issue of VM support, which now apparently requires some more advanced processor features. It's a heavy lift and not one that I expect Debian to maintain for much longer either. They used to do their own white label distribution of RHEL called Scientific Linux with lots of cool add on packages. I'm not sure why they stopped doing so? The normal servers run a mix of RHEL and Alma Linux, Alma chosen over Rocky because of better patching record for security issues and some other things. For the control systems that are ancient CPUs in expensive embedded boxes, Alma and Rocky have the same issue as RHEL in not having working support for older CPUs. Did something change that I am unaware of? I had thought that Redhat had "bought" CentOS" and permanently killed it. Is it alive again? A viable option? "I never thought I'd see a Resonance Cascade, let alone create one..." I do wonder what the Venn Diagram is of people who are running CPUs and systems which are older than 18 years but equally need to run the absolute latest OS version. I would have thought there'd be a desire to not upgrade software to prevent the old "forced obsolete" systems to have to deal with modern day bloat, or the desire to not deal with breaking changes in major OS version upgrades. But on a slightly adjacent thought, presumably CERN is highly optimising their chosen version of Linux for the purpose they are using it for, I'm actually genuinely surprised they haven't basically rolled their own version. Maintaining and running different distributions or major OS versions for different hardware obviously has additional overhead, so you want to avoid that as much as possible. The older hardware is likely still networked, for example, so you definitely have to judge security if not upgrading. Groups like CERN do roll their own OS setups but it saves a lot of continued work if the main configurations start with a base you don't have to build and maintain yourselves. Standard OS builds are more reliable if other You can run Kernel 7.2 on a 486 https://github.com/Sharktastic... [github.com] Which does not help you of the rest of your distro does not run ... You "can" do a lot of things. You "can" play Doom on TI82 calculator. The question was: does it make sense to do so, what are the benefits it brings vs the downsides? S RHEL/CentOS was fine until IBM bought them. THE single most right-on-the-point comment here tonight. That's exactly it. I moved to AlmaLinux almost when it was born, but am always worried that it may get killed, just as CentOS was when IBM bought it and made it the guinea pig between Fedora and RHEL. Cybersecurity is not achieved by running the latest and greatest OS. It's achieved by defence in depth. It's achieved by isolation of systems and control of information. You very much can have an OS out of date and insecure on a network if y I would guess it effects anybody with Linux-based industrial control systems. These can be a lot more expensive than regular computers. Also, Debian is generally not about the "absolute last" version of anything, but rather conservative in its updates. Well this is where I'm coming from. Industrial control systems are not normally kept up to date with the latest OS. For actual control systems they are usually certified combinations of hardware and software. You don't get to upgrade Windows or Linux, what you do is buy a new database server or a new engineering workstation with the new OS on it. The thing is that CERN is probably doing very custom stuff here and needs to do their own thing on industrial hardware. First, Debian can still be run without systemd. Well, mostly and it is getting harder. Second, migration to Devuan is really easy. That said, systemd is mostly a gross violation of all UNIX system design principles, but it should run on older hardware. With RHEL 10 adding more complex stuff like Lightspeed and immutable kernels, it is starting to have a lot more moving parts. RHEL used to be the production choice, but as time went on, there is a lot of oddball stuff, and oddball decisions made. For example, even though Fedora has btrfs, RHEL doesn't have btrfs, even as a -supported- option. (Yes, you can slap ZFS on it, but supported is the big thing). Debian, on the other hand, doesn't have snaps, flatpaks, or other subsystems present by default. It "just works" out of the box.
