Trump Is About to Get the Mail-Ballot Chaos He’s Been Looking For
2026-09-03

Trump Is About to Get the Mail-Ballot Chaos He’s Been Looking For

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. The midterms are two months away, and President Donald Trump has already accomplished a long-sought-after goal: sowing chaos in mail voting. His executive order that taps the U.S. Postal Service and Homeland Security to nationalize U.S. elections elicited a contentious legal battle that culminated in the Supreme Court intervening last week. The justices ruled that—for now—the EO could move forward, but they did not consider the merits of the case, leaving the door wide open for updated lawsuits. And, like clockwork, within days updated litigation was filed, and a Massachusetts judge again issued a 14-day restraining order preventing the order from taking effect. This dizzying back-and-forth puts states in a precarious position: upend their mail-ballot process to comply with the new federal guidance or simply hold off to prevent unnecessary changes and confusion? States and voting rights groups are still engaged in an active legal battle to prevent Trump’s EO from taking effect; for now they have secured two weeks of relief, after which there’s going to be more court action. It is anyone’s guess how this will play out, though U.S. District Court Judge Indira Talwani, who issued the injunctions, said it’s “a likely unconstitutional regulation for which compliance is practically impossible as to the 2026 midterm elections, now little more than two months away.” Since the inception of the United States of America, states have controlled their elections. The Constitution plainly states that “the times, places and manner of holding elections for senators and representatives, shall be prescribed in each state by the legislature thereof,” and this was further codified by Congress in 1993 with the National Voter Registration Act. The Trump administration is well aware of this, but given the president’s well-documented grievances with mail voting, it is actively choosing to ignore the Constitution and undermine U.S. elections by sowing doubt and confusion, something the president has already achieved. According to a whistleblower report this week, USPS is continuing to develop a chaotic new scheme for deciding whether to send out mail ballots that defaults toward refusing to send large batches of ballots based on finding a single unconfirmed error. This scheme may not yet be in effect, but it leaves many states in a sort of limbo, including some that are meant to send out ballots as soon as this week. Through DHS and USPS, Trump’s executive order is attempting to implement three new rules for mail voting: New ballot envelopes that include an intelligent mail barcode and an “Official Election Mail” logo are required in order for USPS to process ballots; states can notify USPS of their intent to use these new mail ballots no fewer than 90 days before an election; and, the most contentious part of the order, state election officials must give USPS a list of every resident in their state who will be sent a mail ballot. It’s not explicitly stated, but the assumption here is that states’ voter lists must match a list of American citizens eligible to vote that DHS also compiled, and USPS has the right to refuse to mail any ballots to voters whose names don’t show up on both lists. If forced to conform to these new rules, all states will face massive costs as they scramble to redo their mail ballots and compile voter lists. They will also need to undertake extensive public-messaging campaigns to inform voters of the new changes. All of this, taken together, will not actually improve election integrity, but it certainly will disenfranchise many legitimate voters. “It’s just taking away from election officials’ ability to run the election they need to run,” Liz Avore, a senior adviser at Voting Rights Lab, a nonpartisan nonprofit that advocates securing and strengthening U.S. elections in all 50 states, told me. To better understand what states are up against, it’s helpful to break down where things now stand in several key states, grouped by mail-ballot status: California, Colorado, Hawaiʻi, Nevada, Oregon, Utah, Vermont, Washington state, and the District of Columbia fall under this category. Huge swaths of the resident populations in these states vote by mail, much higher than the national average. In California, our most populous state, nearly 81 percent of eligible voters voted by mail in the 2024 general election. Meanwhile, everyone in Colorado votes by mail. These states in particular rely on USPS to deliver election mail, and they’ll face higher-than-average costs to change their ballot envelopes to conform with the new USPS standards, given their volume of mail ballots. On the other hand, when it comes to compiling a list of mail voters for USPS, Avore noted that these states are actually better suited than others to share that list. Because so many of their voters already vote by mail, much of the work to distinguish voters who registered to vote by mail from those who did not is already done. “It might be harder for a state like Arizona, for example, that has a lot of voters who vote by mail, but not all of them. They can’t just send over their [complete] voter registration list. They have to submit a subset of voters, but a lot of them.” North Carolina, Massachusetts, and Washington state, at the least, have indicated they’ve already begun printing mail ballots. If forced to comply with the president’s EO, they would need to foot the bill for printing all new ballots, and for potentially addressing confusion from voters who are seeing a new mail-ballot design they’ve never seen before. In Avore’s mind, these states are simply not in a position to comply with the new rules. “It’s too late. They printed it out. They can’t print out all new ballot envelopes before the election,” she said. North Carolina is in an especially tight spot, as it not only already printed mail ballots but began sending them out to voters on Friday. Massachusetts Attorney General Andrea Joy Campbell emphasized this point too, noting that multiple states are scheduled to send mail ballots to their eligible voters, and if the Trump administration prevails in its litigation, “it would be virtually impossible for states to comply with this unlawful rule.” In Washington, Secretary of State Steve Hobbs warned that his counties would have to destroy and replace over 5 million ballot envelopes they have already printed for the November election. Wisconsin, New Hampshire, and Minnesota are among a small group of states with a decentralized election system, in which individual cities and towns, rather than counties, are responsible for administering ballots. This hyperlocal election model results in there being numerous officials who must figure out new rules and regulations, oftentimes with pretty limited funds. These states could face a higher burden of organizing their state’s compliance with new last-minute mail-voting rules and thereby have a higher chance of USPS rejecting their mail ballots. “A state like Wisconsin, where elections are done at the municipal level, they have so many different election administrators, and some of these small jurisdictions have one person that’s working in the elections office,” Avore said. “That’s a huge lift for some of these small, dispersed jurisdictions to figure out how to use USPS’s portal and submit names.” Given Trump’s animus toward mail voting, despite using it himself, his executive order is not surprising. And if it does end up being blocked by the courts, he still managed to etch out a win. That’s because the new set of rules his EO lays out are ripe for violation, as states will inevitably struggle to adhere to new ballot envelope designs and their voter lists are likely to have some discrepancies with DHS’s list. This is all he needs to hook his election fraud claims into, setting off a dizzying array of federal investigations and lawsuits that could successfully convince a cohort of Americans that perhaps our elections should be nationalized—or that potential Democratic gains in the midterms are illegitimate. It’s vital we do not fall for this, especially when considering that Trump’s broader election agenda has actually failed thus far in his presidency—no states have implemented proof of citizenship for registering to vote, and not a single state has eliminated no-excuse mail voting. “For the amount of rhetoric and noise that there’s been around this, the efforts to change the policies have really flopped,” Avore said. “I think that is a testament to the resilience of our elections.” We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction. If you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! For this week’s Amicus, Dahlia Lithwick and Mark Joseph Stern revisit a conversation with Susan Matthews, host of Slow Burn’s latest installment, Becoming Justice Gorsuch. They discuss Gorsuch’s key rulings to date, his unpredictability, and how he’s shaped the court—and our country—for decades to come. In the Amicus bonus episode, Susan again joins Dahlia and Mark to discuss a blockbuster end to the Supreme Court’s 2025–26 term and Gorsuch’s role. They also focus on Gorsuch’s take that the U.S. is a “creedal nation,” founded not on blood but on ideas. The Supreme Court weighed in on Trump’s controversial executive order targeting mail voting and allowed it to proceed—for now. That win lasted approximately three days, until a district judge again issued a restraining order halting the EO from taking effect for 14 days. Robyn Nicole Sanders, a civil rights attorney, explains in Slate how SCOTUS could find itself facing this same case on the merits in a few short weeks. The Trump administration was back at the Supreme Court, this time asking the justices to consider the constitutionality of its restrictions on transgender people serving in the military. Robyn again writes for Slate about how this case stems from yet another presidential executive order, which was promptly challenged by a group of transgender service members and prospective recruits. Trump’s ballroom can proceed, thanks to the Supreme Court’s conservative supermajority, which ruled that the plaintiff suing the administration did not have standing. As Robyn explains in Slate, the majority sidestepped the question weighing the actual legality of the ballroom’s construction, focusing instead on a technical question of standing. But an ICE officer charged in a shooting in Minnesota was arrested in Texas and, rather than being sent to Minnesota for trial, he was simply released 90 days later. Voters Say They Want Younger Politicians but Keep Voting for Old Ones. I Think I Know What’s Really Going On.
2026-09-02

Voters Say They Want Younger Politicians but Keep Voting for Old Ones. I Think I Know What’s Really Going On.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-02

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Here’s a paradox for you: In overwhelming numbers, voters keep telling pollsters that they want upper age limits and term limits for members of Congress. At the same time, they keep voting to reelect long-serving lawmakers who are in their 70s and 80s. Massachusetts offers the latest evidence. Older incumbents of all ideological stripes swept the state’s Democratic primaries Tuesday night. In the race for U.S. Senate, two-term Sen. Ed Markey, an 80-year-old progressive, easily dispatched the more moderate Rep. Seth Moulton, 47. Elsewhere, Reps. Richard Neal and Stephen Lynch, who are both in their 70s and have collectively been in office for more than six decades, fended off younger and leftier challengers. So what’s really going on? Here are a few theories. As the saying goes, age is but a number. Yet voters keep telling us it’s actually a lot more than that. At first glance, Markey’s win would seem to break a recent streak of older incumbents falling to more youthful candidates. So far this year, the losers include Rep. Adriano Espaillat of New York (71), Rep. Shri Thanedar of Michigan (71), and Rep. Diana DeGette of Colorado (69). But Markey’s victory also fits a different pattern: In all three of those earlier races, the elder lawmaker lost to a more outspokenly progressive rival. And for Massachusetts Democrats contemplating who they wanted as their next senator, ideology was clearly more important than age. When Moulton launched his campaign, he downplayed any real policy differences with Markey. “Sen. Markey is a good man, but it’s time for a new generation of leadership,” he said in his announcement video. Markey, though, hammered the issues where they did diverge—including the debate over transgender rights. After Kamala Harris lost in 2024, a defeat some Democrats attributed in part to a viral pro-Trump ad that argued “Kamala is for they/them, President Trump is for you,” Moulton expressed opposition to trans athletes playing in women’s sports. “I have two little girls, I don’t want them getting run over on a playing field by a male or formerly male athlete,” he told the New York Times. Moulton later retreated from those remarks (“I’m sorry if my comments hurt you,” he told the trans community last month), but Markey sought to make them stick. “Trans rights are human rights,” the senator said recently. In a preelection poll from the University of Massachusetts Amherst, two-thirds of likely Democratic primary voters said they were less likely to support a candidate who had opposed trans rights, and about the same share said they trusted Markey to better represent them on the issues they cared about. So, going forward, it’s probably better to think of age as a factor, but rarely the factor, in how voters approach candidates. Moulton’s drubbing has some progressives crowing that new ideas, not just fresh faces, are what Democrats need to compete. How, then, to explain the survival of Neal and Lynch, two longtime members of Congress who put down progressive challenges from first-time candidates last night? One answer is straightforward: Politicians who seek reelection almost always win. Nationwide, 95 percent of all incumbents reclaimed their seats in 2024. Journalists have showered ink on the anti-establishment fervor that’s supposedly sweeping the Democratic Party, buoyed by voters furious over its longtime leaders’ failure to prevent Donald Trump’s return. But even in that treacherous environment, only seven sitting House Democrats, a tiny fraction of those running for reelection, have lost renomination so far this year. Lynch and Neal both won relatively narrowly, which may suggest some frustration with their extensive tenures or more centrist records (or both). But there’s clearly a risk of overstating Democrats’ unhappiness with their party’s old guard—and of mapping that anger too neatly onto ideology. Polling is an imperfect instrument, and asking voters whether they favor a given candidate or policy doesn’t always tell you why they feel the way they do. So another possible explanation for the apparent paradox over older politicians is that age, at least by itself, isn’t really what voters are worried about. The better term for what’s on their minds may instead be health, both cognitive and physical. The clearest example is what happened last month, when Rep. John Larson, a 78-year-old Democrat who has represented Connecticut since 1999, lost his primary to a younger challenger. But Larson wasn’t just old and established. He had a complex partial seizure while delivering an on-camera speech on the House floor last year, stopping midsentence before continuing to speak in a slow and halting voice for more than 30 seconds. Larson’s office later attributed the seizure to a congenital heart issue, adding that he’d been prescribed medication to help. But it’s not all that shocking that voters declined to return him to Congress, opting instead for a 47-year-old former mayor who campaigned on generational change. The health-over-age dynamic may also explain Markey’s resilience. As even some of Moulton’s supporters acknowledged, the 80-year-old senator still looks spry and sounds with it. (After a recent rally, Markey’s campaign blasted out video of the senator shooting hoops.) Markey would be 86 at the end of his next term, the same age Joe Biden would’ve been had he won and finished a second term, but voters evidently saw them differently. In a Suffolk University/Boston Globe poll last month, only a third of Massachusetts primary voters said Markey’s age made them less inclined to support him. When Biden launched his reelection bid, on the other hand, even most Democrats told pollsters he was too old for another term. They may have been alarmed less by the year on Biden’s birth certificate than by his apparent physical frailty, his softening voice, and questions about his mental acuity. OK, time to switch gears. For your evening enjoyment, why not: Take a break with this movie review: We regret to inform you that Raygun is back. The Aussie breakdancer who went viral in 2024 for her cringingly bad performance at the Summer Olympics in Paris is the subject of an infuriating new Netflix documentary about the whole ordeal. Slate culture writer Nadira Goffe watched it so you don’t have to. Soothe your inner child with some nostalgic tunes: When Rebecca Knight became a parent, she vowed that her kids wouldn’t listen to Raffi. But she quickly realized the error of her ways, thanks to a Laura Marling album of Raffi covers. (If you want to learn more about Raffi’s singular status in the world of kids’ music, listen to this 2023 episode of Working in which the artist breaks down his creative decisions.) Get in the back-to-school spirit: It can be hard to adjust to the end of summer vacation. But there is one small part of the new school year that is guaranteed to enthrall students, parents, and teachers alike. Slate staff writer Heather Schwedel investigates why lamination—a hallmark of an organized classroom—has such a hold on both kids and grown-ups. Give your brain a workout: Who is “Wonder Woman’s godly enemy” (four letters)? Which city “has a Jewish population that is neither Sephardic or Ashkenazi” (four letters)? Test your wordplay skills with today’s crossword. Alas, writing this newsletter has exhausted my wordplay skills, so I’m off to walk the dog instead. Enjoy the rest of your night! The GOP Made a Huge Miscalculation. Republican Voters Are Furious.
2026-09-02

The GOP Made a Huge Miscalculation. Republican Voters Are Furious.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-02

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Once again, government officials are trying to restrict marijuana products around the United States. And once again, Americans of all political stripes are pissed off—plausibly enough to shake up this fall’s midterms. At the federal level, Republican lawmakers and the Trump administration are attempting to rectify a huge mistake they made last November, when they passed an ill-advised bill that, if allowed to go into effect, would all but smother the United States’ flowering hemp industry. In red states like Iowa, Kansas, and Ohio—where polls have shown Democratic momentum in the gubernatorial races—promising candidates are insisting on the need to finally, belatedly legalize it. A bid in blue Massachusetts to have voters approve a rollback of the state’s recreational industry faces overwhelming popular opposition. And the anger is especially boiling down in Texas, where even Joe Rogan is warning that GOP intransigence on weed could well cause the Lone Star State to vote Democratic in the upcoming midterms. Since the 2024 election, conservative politicians have attempted to stoke reefer madness as a backlash to the legalization movement’s state-by-state victories. Their justifications, which seized on legitimate concerns over the unfortunate prevalence of misleadingly packaged hemp-derived candies stocked in unlicensed gas stations and smoke shops, have pivoted away from children’s safety to old, tired arguments for outright prohibition. And this fall, they’re likely to see their concerted counteractions fall flat—even, and especially, in the areas they’d least expect. Texas, which does not appear to have learned from the uproar its lawmakers inspired the previous time they attempted a weed crackdown, provides an incisive example. In late July, a long-pending state rule went into effect that outlawed all sales of certain smokable THC products derived from hemp, including milder and medicinal pot alternatives. These derivatives, which were federally legalized by the 2018 farm bill, allowed a limited lineup of retail cannabis options in a state where medical marijuana is legal but recreational bud is not. (Individual Texas cities where residents voted to either legalize or decriminalize the plant have seen those efforts preempted by municipal and state authorities.) The restrictions—which date back to a 2021 Health Services Department rule imposing the harshest drug-law regulations on THC derivatives—are newly enforceable, thanks to a January ruling from the state Supreme Court allowing Texas to fine or jail retailers who’ve established themselves in the hemp business. If they’re not licensed medical providers, they’re able to sell only standard delta-9 flowers with less than 0.3 percent THC content. It’s yet another burden on Texan pot enthusiasts, who’ve already had to deal with the fallout from an attempted ban on smokable hemp and another law, passed about a year ago, that illegalized the sale (though not the possession) of THC vape pens. Democratic Senate nominee James Talarico has already seized on the issue, referring to these harsh rollbacks as an example of “the nanny state at its worst.” His Republican opponent, Attorney General Ken Paxton, has notably pivoted his rhetorical tack from outspoken denunciation of legal cannabis to professing to not know any details about his own administration’s rules. A county judge has even complained that Paxton’s office is not responding in a timely manner to queries about how the state wishes to proceed in court, now that multiple hemp businesses have filed lawsuits. Already staring down constituent anger over wildlife destruction (from border-wall buildouts) and noise pollution (from data center projects), the Texas GOP is reckoning with the very real possibility that once-loyal voters will follow through on their public threats to vote blue this round. The party has also likely noticed that Talarico’s stance on hemp is not hurting him in the polls. Another concern for regular Texans: Even the low-THC hemp exempted from the state rules may soon be illegal to sell nationally, thanks to the congressional bill signed last year that, if allowed to go into effect, would forbid the growth of crops with more than 0.3 percent THC. From the moment it was introduced, that federal crackdown inspired plenty of jitters from the Trump White House, as well as Republicans who represent areas that have largely benefited from Congress’ willingness to relax historic restraints on hemp cultivation. (This legal stigmatization of the widely useful crop, one of the many unfortunate consequences of the war on drugs, began to unravel by 2014.) In agriculture-heavy conservative states like Iowa, Kansas, and Texas, farmers, entrepreneurs, and everyday conservatives have profited from the billions of dollars in business and tax revenue unlocked by the industry, which also opened up paths for veterans, seniors, and disabled residents to make use of salves for chronic pain. It was perhaps a belated recognition of these consequences—along with some phone calls from President Donald Trump himself—that inspired the GOP-controlled Senate to delay the implementation of the new federal hemp standards, pushing back their enactment date from election season to mid-December instead. On Tuesday, the House of Representatives followed suit. (In a subsequent statement to Slate, the Hemp Beverage Alliance wrote that its industry “looks forward to working with Congress to create a regulatory framework that protects public health, keeps products away from minors, and permanently removes the bad actors that sell untested, high-potency, copycat gummies and candies that target children.”) In a manner historically unusual for GOP presidents, Trump has helped lead the push to reschedule the classification of cannabis at the national level, in a move that will continue to help hemp enthusiasts experiment and figure out ways to develop and sell this crop in a safe and scalable manner. But the chief executive—who’s extremely unpopular for a host of other reasons, including economic mismanagement and immigration-law abuses—is not on the ballot this fall. The people on his side who are on the ballot are those who’ve historically echoed the same baseless fearmongering that dominated 1980s-era messaging around marijuana. But it’s not the ’80s anymore, and even Republican voters understand that, along with the many other independent, on-the-fence Americans who may have swung toward Trump in 2024 but are far more skeptical of his party in 2026. The people from outside the base, those who aren’t too attached to partisan politics and enjoy the freedoms that allow them to burn one, are now feeling burned themselves—and they’re about to make that clear in November. Nvidia buys Hugging Face for $12.9B, promises not to squeeze too hard
2026-09-03

Nvidia buys Hugging Face for $12.9B, promises not to squeeze too hard

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
GPU giant pledges to keep the model hub open to the entire AI ecosystem Nvidia has agreed to buy AI developer platform Hugging Face for $12.9 billion, tightening its grip on the ecosystem surrounding open models. Following weeks of industry chatter, Nvidia confirmed Thursday it had entered into a definitive agreement to buy the model-hosting hub, sometimes described as a GitHub for AI developers. The transaction is expected to close in the first half of 2027, subject to regulatory approval and other customary closing conditions. Open foundation models and the applications built on them drive demand for the accelerators and software Nvidia sells. Owning one of the main platforms through which developers discover and distribute those models brings the GPU giant closer to the workloads running on its hardware. Nvidia said the purchase will give Hugging Face additional resources and support developers building, sharing, and deploying open models and applications. It pledged to keep the platform open and allow model makers, developers, and users to access models and datasets of their choosing. "Open models let startups, businesses, universities and public institutions build on advanced capabilities without training every model from scratch. They enable organizations to match the right model to the right job," Nvidia chief Jensen Huang wrote in a blog announcing the move. "That is how AI can advance safely, strengthen cybersecurity and sovereignty, accelerate innovation, and reach factories, hospitals, farms, classrooms and Main Street businesses around the world." In a post on X, Hugging Face co-founder and CEO Clément Delangue said that open source AI is at an inflection point. "Thanks to the community, we've shown that it can be a complement, and even an alternative, to closed-source APIs. But for it to happen at larger scale, it needs more compute, more support, more collaboration and more visibility. That's why we went to talk to Jensen, who offered to do exactly that with us," he explained. But the move could also be seen as the industry giant further consolidating its central position in the AI industry. "Nvidia has already built an extended ecosystem through GPUs, CUDA, networking, inference software, and AI frameworks. Hugging Face will give it a stronger position at the developer, model distribution, and community layers, which will strategically help to shape where AI workloads are built and deployed," says Forrester Research principal analyst Charlie Dai. Enterprise users of Hugging Face should be alert to any shift in its open stance, he cautioned. "As Hugging Face's value comes from neutrality, Nvidia is likely to preserve openness initially. Enterprises should watch for future shifts rather than immediate disruption, while risk assessment around deeper integration with Nvidia tooling, runtimes, and optimization frameworks will be necessary over time." It will also likely renew concerns over the financial stability of the whole AI ecosystem, as a small number of very large companies and investors seem to be involved in many of the deals keeping the industry's wheels turning. Nigel Green, CEO of financial consultants deVere Group, recently compared China's AI ecosystem with America's "dangerously circular" trade. "For weeks I've been flagging that too much of the AI trade in the US is financing itself, with the same capital moving between a tight circle of suppliers, lenders and customers and getting booked as growth every time it changes hands. "A supplier invests in a customer, that customer spends the investment buying the supplier's own hardware, and the identical dollars get counted as fresh revenue at every stop along the loop," he commented. As The Register reported last month, Nvidia is increasingly using the huge profits it has made out of AI to shore up the foundations of the entire ecosystem. The GPU giant is increasingly becoming the central pillar of the AI industry, holding it all together. ® But investors hear CRM giant is now in 'refinement mode,' picking models more carefully GPU giant pledges to keep the model hub open to the entire AI ecosystem PARTNER CONTENT: Platform engineering won the argument. Cybercrooks trawl Fishbrain to net password hashes
2026-09-03

Cybercrooks trawl Fishbrain to net password hashes

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Armed with password hashes and salts, attackers could already be kraken those creds Cybercriminals have reeled in password hashes and corresponding salts belonging to users of popular fishing app Fishbrain, opening the door to cracking attempts. Fishbrain AB, which says its eponymous app serves more than 20 million anglers, disclosed the August 19 breach to the California Attorney General's Office this week. The unknown perpetrators helped themselves to a trawl of user data, including names, dates of birth, email addresses, phone numbers, Fishbrain usernames, country information, password hashes, and salts. "Fishbrain passwords were not stored in plaintext; however, Fishbrain has determined that the compromised password hashes for some users may be susceptible to being decoded," the company said in its disclosure [PDF]. It added: "If you use your Fishbrain password for any other online accounts, you should promptly update those passwords and any associated security questions or answers. "You should also take other appropriate steps to protect any online accounts that use the same username or email address and password combination. We recommend using a strong, unique password for each of your accounts." With the hashes and salts in hand, attackers can make password guesses using their own hardware until they potentially recover the original credentials. Whether those attempts succeed depends on the strength of each password and the hashing algorithm Fishbrain used, which the company did not disclose. Fishbrain did not comment on the scale of the breach or how many of its claimed 20 million-plus users were affected. The Register asked Fishbrain for more information. After discovering the intrusion and conducting an initial forensic investigation, Fishbrain patched the vulnerability and reset every user's password. Customers must create a new one the next time they log in. Fishbrain also said it "restricted access to the affected environment," strengthened its security controls, and initiated "a broader review of our data security measures" while the investigation continues. Fisherfolk should also keep an eye out for phisherfolk using the stolen personal data to bait follow-on attacks. ® But investors hear CRM giant is now in 'refinement mode,' picking models more carefully GPU giant pledges to keep the model hub open to the entire AI ecosystem PARTNER CONTENT: Platform engineering won the argument. Hohem’s tiny steadycam has a removable action cam
2026-09-03

Hohem’s tiny steadycam has a removable action cam

The Verge | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
The Eyepic can capture smooth stabilized 4K video or more dynamic footage by detaching and wearing its camera module. The Eyepic can capture smooth stabilized 4K video or more dynamic footage by detaching and wearing its camera module. Hohem is trying something new with its Eyepic handheld stabilized camera to differentiate it from existing offerings from companies like DJI and Insta360. At its core, the camera is functionally similar to devices like the Osmo Pocket 4 and Insta360 Luna Ultra with a rotating preview screen and a small camera module stabilized by a three-axis gimbal. But the Eyepic borrows an idea popularized by recent action cams by letting you detach the magnetic camera module and use it as a tiny standalone camera you can mount or wear. The Eyepic captures 4K video at up to 60fps in a vertical or horizontal orientation that can be changed by rotating the 2.51-inch touchscreen display on its grip, which also includes a joystick for manually adjusting framing and a lever for changing the zoom level. Details on battery life, built-in storage, tracking capabilities, and pricing haven’t been shared yet. To capture more dynamic hands-free footage, the Eyepic camera can be used on its own clipped to clothing, worn using an included lanyard, or magnetically attached to compatible surfaces. The tiny action cam lacks a screen of its own but streams a live 1080p preview to the Eyepic’s touchscreen that can be used to preview framing or remotely adjust settings up to 32 feet away. The grip and gimbal aren’t water-friendly, but the action cam module is completely waterproof to a depth of just over 32 feet. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The Verge
How Sonos rebooted itself
2026-09-03

How Sonos rebooted itself

The Verge | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
CEO Tom Conrad on cleaning up Sonos’ app redesign mess and turning a speaker company into a software platform. If you buy something from a Verge link, Vox Media may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. If you buy something from a Verge link, Vox Media may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. Today, I’m talking with Tom Conrad, the CEO of Sonos. Tom and I have known each other for a long time — he was the chief technology officer of Pandora, VP of product at Snap, and the chief product officer of Quibi. He was also on the board at Sonos during its disastrous 2024 app redesign that threatened to crash the entire brand. Tom took over as interim CEO to right the ship during that period, and then took the permanent role just about a year ago. As you’ll hear him say, he thinks Sonos is fully out of that period now, and on to its next chapter with a huge product and platform launch called Sonos 27. Verge subscribers, don’t forget you get exclusive access to ad-free Decoder wherever you get your podcasts. Head here. Not a subscriber? You can sign up here. I mean, come on, you all listen to the show. That is all the most Decoder bait imaginable. I have been dying to have Tom on the show to talk about how the board decided to move on from previous CEO Patrick Spence, how he diagnosed the issues inside the company, and what decisions he made to fix them. Those fixes involved a lot of big changes: to the company’s structure, its product lineup, and how it thinks about itself and what its product fundamentally is. That’s that new platform, Sonos 27, which you’ll hear Tom call a new foundation for the company that makes every Sonos product work better as part of a system. There’s a lot here, including how AI assistants can work across the system, the idea of having a mesh network of little computers in your home disguised as speakers, and of course, the testy relationship between Sonos and Google, which means Google’s Gemini assistant isn’t available on Sonos devices. Oh, and of course we got into the weeds about smart home standards, because I am a smart home nerd and simply cannot help myself. But to his credit, Tom was willing to nerd out with me, and I think this conversation is pretty fun because of it. This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity. Tom Conrad, you’re the CEO of Sonos. Welcome to Decoder. Thank you. As they say, it’s so great to be here. I am just going to say, finally, I have been asking you to be on the show for at least a year, if not before. I think the first time I met you was in the back of a car at CES when you were at Pandora. [Laughs] We were little babies. That car was driven by a human driver. That’s how far back it was. And then you took a turn through Quibi. You and I talked through that, and now you’re at Sonos. As you know, I’m a huge fan of Sonos. We had your predecessor, Patrick Spence, on the show a bunch of times. Many things have happened, so I just want to start at the very beginning of this. You’re about a year into being the permanent CEO of Sonos. You have a bunch of news to announce. Just tell us what you think Sonos is today. What is your conception of this company? When I came in the door, one of my earliest observations was that the company behaved like it was 100 percent hardware, but we’re really 100 percent hardware and 100 percent software. And if you get either one of those even a little bit wrong, the impact is tremendous. So how does that show up really day-to-day? It was about returning the company to the idea that Sonos doesn’t make these individual products, but we really make one thing — we make the Sonos system. It all has to hang together in a cohesive, interesting, and valuable way, or we’re just not doing the work. There’s been a bunch of basic blocking and tackling, of course, both operationally and in the work that we’re doing in the product family. But at the highest level, returning the company to the idea that we are a system for audio in the home has been the highest-level mantra. When you say come into the company, do you mean as a board member or as the CEO? I mean, let’s start the starter pistol in January of 2025, 18 months ago, when I stepped in first as interim CEO and then was lucky enough six months later to be asked to take the job more permanently. The reason I asked that question is that you obviously have two different views of the company in different roles. We did have former CEO Patrick Spence on several occasions, and he said something very similar to me when he first took over — that Sonos was stuck in its ways. It was really focused on very, very slow iterations of hardware. He tried very hard to push the metabolism of the company to be much faster, and he talked about that very openly with me. That led eventually to what I think is a moment of true crisis: the app launch. It was a disaster. The system stopped working. That’s when you came in to right the ship. Tell me about that gap. You came in, and you said this company thinks it’s a hardware company. That’s what your predecessor had been saying, but it went sideways in big, meaningful ways. Tell me about that moment and why you think it was the same conception even when you came in. So as you describe, I’ve been involved with the company going back almost 20 years. We brought Pandora to Sonos in 2007, I think, and I was able to get to know the company as a partner before I got to know it as a board member. I came onto the board in 2016 right as Patrick became CEO. So from the vantage point of a board member, I got to participate in Patrick’s chapter. I guess the way I would characterize his high-order thinking about the company in those early days was that, as you described, he wanted to turn the company into a product execution machine with an emphasis on hardware products. We call them NPIs, or new product introductions. In the chapter before him, it was sometimes years between new Sonos products. Spence said, “Look, we’re going to make a company that knows how to ship a couple of great new products a year.” And he did that. I mean, the company has reliably shipped a couple of great products, hardware products, every year, for close to a decade now. Sometimes, when you energize an organization around an idea, there are unintended consequences. I think one unintended consequence of that framing was that the company became very focused on the individual products, one at a time. Even as a board member, I received Roam as a portable speaker with a certain amount of battery life, waterproofing, and Bluetooth connectivity. I didn’t really think about it as a member of the Sonos system. Even with the software work that we did, and with every product that we make, there is a tremendous amount of software effort and innovation that goes into them. But even the software that we made was very focused on the individual NPIs. “What can we do in software to differentiate Move 2 from other speakers in that category?” What was hard to do, and what was hard to prioritize in the company, was horizontal slices of software experiences that benefited all of the platform. So, in a way, I think some of that debt accumulated, and the company thought that the antidote was to recreate the app experience. And as you know, we made a bunch of mistakes at that moment. I’m really proud of the fact that I think we’re well beyond that chapter now, but we could have been much more thoughtful about how we qualified that product in the real world. We could have been much more thoughtful about how we rolled it out to customers. We could have, candidly, I think, examined the original impetus in the first place. It was very Sonos-motivated. We wanted to put the app on a modern architecture to make us more nimble. In my chapter at Sonos, I’m really trying to bring it all back to how we can focus on the customer first. I’m so inspired by talking to people on social media and in my inbox, and people in the industry, like you, about how we show up and solve the real problems that are happening in real households. I’m so excited about the things we’re going to talk to you about today because I think they’re really an answer to many of those questions. If you’re a listener, you know I’m familiar with Tom. The man’s on Threads doing customer support every day. And actually, I should not take advantage of this. I hold myself back from asking you for direct customer support all the time. Anytime. The way that you can tell it’s actually me and not our crackerjack social team is that I often will let a Thread die before I should or fail to follow up with someone. So if we were executing perfectly on that, you would know it was fake Tom, but it’s real Tom, and I do it when I can, but sometimes imperfectly. At least it’s not an AI avatar Tom. That’s when we know it’s going to go sideways. I know you’re a Decoder fan, so I’m going to make you sit in this for just one more moment because I find all of this to be really fascinating. The app shipped, and the feedback was immediately and intensely negative. Many, many things were broken. Speakers weren’t working at weddings. This is a time of massive crisis. The company goes to resolve it, but then you’re on the board, and it’s clear that there’s going to be a leadership transition, that Patrick needs to go, and we need a new CEO. You’re going to be the interim CEO. Tell us about that process. Very, very few people will ever be in that kind of process or get insight into that process. What happened? What were the steps? Well, I guess you start by saying that the last thing as a board that you want to do is to remove a CEO, particularly one who had as many successes as Patrick had. But I think as we looked at the company’s progress recovering from the app crisis, and frankly the tenor of the conversation inside the company, we just decided that for this kind of step-function improvement we needed on some of these dimensions, we had to make some changes in leadership, and that included a CEO change. It was a wild experience for me because, I’ll tell you, at no point in the decade leading up to that day in January did I imagine that I might end up the CEO of Sonos. But in the process, the board asked if I’d be willing to step in on an interim basis, and it was an easy yes for me. It’s a cliché, but I’m a customer, and I love this product. There was a Sonos Move in the room when my daughter was born. You talked about weddings. I was watching friends and colleagues on the internet, frustrated by their experience. I wanted to come in and fix it. Of course, as a board member, I felt some real culpability in it. So [I was] thrilled at the chance to come in. When you’re an interim CEO, the assignment is really to keep the wheels on. The assignment at Sonos specifically was to right the ship. So during the period when the board was doing their comprehensive search for the person that they would ultimately decide was the right choice for the company, it was really easy for me to show up and just do that hard work of trying to get the company back on track. While I never imagined that I would land in this seat, I feel so blessed that it happened. I do love this job and continue to love the company and the team, and I’m so excited about the things we’re doing for our customers. Very few people are going to get an interview to become the CEO of Sonos. I imagine at some point you went from being the interim CEO to wanting to become the permanent CEO. What was that process like? Did you write a memo? Was there a pitch deck? What were the steps? Yeah, so there were a couple of pitch moments that the board asked me, completely reasonably, to engage in the process of applying for the job in the same way that all of the other talented candidates that they spoke to engaged. And so I took a personality test. Oh, yeah. One of the letter things, sure. They wanted to know what they were getting. Do they know that that is basically just like astrology? Someone should tell them. You know what? Yep, yep. I presented my vision for the company more than once, and I had the incredible advantage of being inside the four walls of the company and deeply understanding what was working and what wasn’t, and the things that we needed to do to get to this next chapter. It was a period of intense, intense work for me and everybody around me at the company, and more than a little anxiety about how it was going to turn out. It was so fun, though, to get the call and then to just immediately be able to pivot at least a fraction of the work from the entirely tactical to the much more strategic, to begin to talk about what kind of company we are going to build together in this next chapter. When it was announced that you would become the permanent CEO, even when you were the interim CEO, my reaction was, “Oh, this makes sense.” To use a football analogy, football teams hire defensive head coaches and offensive head coaches, and they come from one side of the ball or the other. It was just obvious: “Oh, this company needs a software CEO, and Tom is a software guy.” You’re describing a situation where obviously it’s both. You’ve got hardware and software together, but that was the light that went off in my head — “Oh, this is a software person coming in to fix a big software problem.” Is that how you saw it initially? Yeah, my career started with selling personal computers in 1986 or something, at the original Micro Center. If you’ve ever been to a Micro Center, it’s one of the few remaining big-box retailers for computers. So at the original Micro Center in Columbus, Ohio, at 16 years old, I walked into the CEO’s office and said, “I want to work here.” So from my earliest days, I was sitting with early adopters of personal computers and watching their struggles to understand the technology and put it to use in their lives. I became an engineer and a product designer out of a desire to help people learn to use these products. And that’s really what’s motivated me through my entire career: how do I help teams make great experiences? So while it’s true that I’m a software person, 35 years in the business, my identity really is as a product designer. And I’d like to think that the board imagined that, yes, they needed a software person, but they needed somebody who could holistically look at the opportunity across hardware and software and help the team get there. You appear to be through it now. There’s a big stack of announcements here, including MCP integration. I’ve got a lot of questions about all that. When you became the permanent CEO 18 months ago, you said you immediately shifted strategy. What brought that into focus for you? “Oh, this is actually a software platform with hardware components”? Was that instinct? Was it research? Was it, “I’m getting through this app process, and that is becoming revealed to me”? How did that work? I think by disposition I’m a fairly right-brained person, and I have this intuitive feeling — creative, or whatever the words are, not as quantitative. But my first job out of college was at Apple, and I think even from the early days when I was there, we talked about the intersection of the arts and humanities and the sciences. I think one lesson of my career has been that you really do have an obligation to bring both a quant and a qual assessment to most things that pass your desk. So the development of our strategy was very much like that. Yes, there was an intuitive sense about what the right answer for Sonos was that was built up from my experience as a customer by observing our customers and talking to them through the years. A piece of that is a perspective about where the puck is going, of course. But I do have access to piles and piles and piles of data about how our products behave in the real world, how our customers interact with them, what they use, what they don’t use, and lots of people around me in the organization who can bring that quantitative framing. I think one advantage I had over other CEO candidates was that I could bring some of that horsepower to bear on the story that I shared with the board about my vision for the future of the company. You’ve made some changes. It’s time for the Decoder questions. You have restructured the company. There have been a few rounds of layoffs. How big is Sonos today? So the company that I stepped into was divided into business units. We had a team that was focused on home. We had a team that was focused on the professional opportunity, a team that was focused on home theater, and so on. While there’s something to be said for P&L responsibilities, et cetera, et cetera, et cetera, there’s something to be said for that approach at a certain scale. I think that my appraisal was that we were sub-scale for the inefficiencies that creep in. We have the almost guaranteed duplication of effort across the silos. What’s more, if you imagine that the product is really the system, the idea that your portables team sits far away from your home theater team, and sits far away from your plugged-in speakers team, is almost surely going to run counter to the kind of progress that you’re trying to motivate. And so very quickly, within three weeks of my arrival, we reorganized the company into a functional model. So my leadership team today, of course, has a finance organization, a legal organization, a sales organization, and a marketing organization. But the product team now shows up as represented by hardware, software, and global supply chain operations, so leaders across each of those things. And what that allows us to do is to assemble cross-functional teams, or matrix-style teams, to invest in the initiatives that are of the highest priority to our customers and to the business at any given moment. And we can flex that up and down and side to side as our needs change. We might, for an interval, be very focused on this part of the portfolio over here, and then we might be able to move to focusing on a different corner of the portfolio. No one has to get a new boss, and people can fluidly move from these things and get new opportunities to learn. It just strikes me as a much more appropriate organizational structure for our current stage. You’re describing the shift from a divisional structure to a vastly more functional structure. Everyone comes up, and there’s one big product meeting. You obviously make a lot of software. I’ve described you as a software CEO. It seems like the software industry is in a moment of existential crisis about how software is made, about what a product manager versus an engineer versus a designer do. Are you still in the classic structures of building software? Or has AI completely upended what you’re doing? No, we’re as deep in this as anyone. What’s been really fun for me over the course of the last year is to watch the software engineering team go from thoughtful skepticism to pretty deep embrace, if I’m honest. Some of that is on the back of just how dramatically the tools have evolved over the course of the last 12 months, certainly. I think almost everyone in the industry went home at the holidays at the tail end of 2025, coming into 2026, and came back just gobsmacked by how much progress the frontier labs were making on agentic coding, and it’s been a real accelerant for us. As complex as this transition is, I’m really excited about being able to help our engineers navigate what’s a pretty fundamental sea change in the way that this kind of infrastructure gets built, and it’s fun to get to be a part of that at this moment. Did you break up the classic PM, designer, and engineer triad? Or is that still the core building block? We still have all three, but it blurs particularly as the designers and the product managers can be prototypers at a really high level with incredible efficiency. It’s exciting to see how quickly they can communicate their ideas now in a medium that just didn’t exist at all for them a year ago. We’re at the turn, right? You came in, you restructured the company, you identified a new thesis for the company. Today you’re here to talk about all the new news. Do you think you’re through it? Do you think the existing customers have forgiven you for the app mess? Do you think the brand has been restored? Yeah, as we’ve been saying, I think that there’s the quant and there’s the qual. The quant is green across the board. Performance, reliability, customer care interactions, social tone, NPS — these things are all restored to levels that we enjoyed before any of that happened. On the qualitative side, I love the fact that I can show up on Reddit or on social media broadly and engage with our customers and have a constructive conversation about things that they’re excited that we’re doing, and things where we’re still disappointing an individual customer on some dimension. I think that at some level, maybe this game is never over; you’re just always trying to polish off the remaining rough edges of everything that you do. So I have a real critic’s eye when I look at the work that we do, and if I ever let that slip, a customer will remind me of where the work remains. And so, yes, I think in every measurable way it’s behind us, but it’s a “never forget” chapter for the company. That brings me to the other Decoder question: how do you make decisions? What’s your framework? Well, I don’t think you bring just one framework to every decision. So we talk about, for example, product decisions. As an idea moves through its lifecycle from its days as an early little sapling that you have to care and tend to, through final mass production for a hardware object, and out into the channels, I think how you think about [each of those] decisions is different. So in the earliest days, I really tried to protect the creativity of the company and to not let the antibodies of doubt and critique erode an idea before it’s had a chance to express itself in a way that anybody could get excited about. That means creating room for risk-taking, creativity, qualitative assessment, and prototyping before you get to the details of the business case and the hard trade-offs that you make on a journey from an idea to something you can actually ship. But of course, as the programs roll along and they take on more and more financial investment and organizational risk, as more and more people aggregate into the product, you become, I think, just naturally much more quantitative in your analysis of the decisions. And you ask the teams to bring a really thoughtful, detailed description of the puts and takes and the risks associated with it, and you use that to help you make your decisions. But maybe on top of all of that, you just have to hire great people. I mean, you just do, and you have to put them on teams that are an appropriate size for the task at hand. I think it’s going to be very, very difficult for any CEO or any leadership team to make great decisions if the people who aren’t bringing the ideas themselves aren’t great. So I suppose it starts with the right people in the seats. The reason I ask that question in that way is that, I think, the decision you made that is most emblematic to me of changing from a divisional structure with individual P&Ls to a more functional structure, and thinking about the platform as a whole, was the decision to kill a product that I believe was called Pinewood. It was a set-top box that was going to integrate all the streaming services. This was well reported by former Verge reporter Chris Welch, who’s now at Bloomberg. The thing that really struck me about that was if you had a division that was focused on soundbars, the only place that division could go was to compete with Roku. And all the reporting that we had before suggested that the soundbar division was growing because the attach rate for soundbars with new TVs is 100 percent. Everyone knows the speakers in their TVs are bad. It’s a great business, and we’re for sure the best product in the category. So if you want to grow, which is the big thing, you’re going to be like, “How do we grow? And you say, “Well, I got to beat Roku.” And then now you’re just off doing that. You came in, and you just ended that, and I thought, “Oh, this is not just a decision to end a product line; this is also a decision about the strategy of the company. Because if you’re going to go compete with Roku, you’ve got to be a different company.” That’s my view of it. Is that how you saw it? Like so many things, these decisions do have more than one dimension that you evaluate. A big part of that decision, candidly, was that we were spread too thin across too many things, and I didn’t believe that we could be exceptional at all of them. I wanted to return us to focus on the thing that allows us to be most differentiated in the competitive set, and that’s the incredible systemness of the product, the audio experience that we can deliver, and attending to what, at the time, was a very long list of things that our customers were explicitly asking us for. I think if we were, putatively, actually working on a set-top box at that moment, another thing that I might have wondered about was whether our desire to make this particular product came from our customer or did it come from some sort of internally sourced sense of business opportunity? And I think companies get in a lot of trouble when the thing that comes first is the model and the thing that comes second is the product. I certainly feel a sense of obligation to constantly be evaluating new business opportunities for the company, new business models for the company, sure. But I want them to always start from a place of what we really see in the market from customers. I’m not sure that they were saying we want a high-end streaming box that is somehow the love child of an Apple TV and a Roku. Although that love child… I would be very worried about that love child. For 20 years, we’ve given all of our hardware objects names: Roam, Arc, Beam, Ace, Era. But we’ve never named the software platform — the thing that takes these collections of products and creates a consistent whole. So in some ways, the big news that we’re sharing right now is that the software platform has a name called Sonos 27, and it has a bunch of promises that it makes to customers in its nuts and bolts. It’s an ecosystem platform that allows a huge heterogeneous world of services to come together in your home. It’s a system that has a deep technological layer that tunes the audio for every part of your household through a system we call Trueplay. It’s fundamentally built on a kind of fabric that brings all of the products together into a cohesive whole. And architecturally, it’s one of the things that’s really, really unique about what we’re doing. Our products in your home really find each other on the local network and create something that is truly where the whole is greater than the sum of the parts. Tell me about the idea of naming the operating system Sonos 27. It’s obviously the middle of 2026. It obviously tracks Apple’s naming scheme. This very much implies there’ll be a Sonos 28 one year from now, in August of 2027. It does. I think from the beginning we knew that we wanted to give it a name, and we knew that we wanted the name to be able to express this idea that Sonos does evolve. And for many, many years, that evolution was to the great benefit of our customers. We had lossless audio for Apple. We had high-fidelity audio for Spotify. We had AirPlay. We had Alexa. Through the years, we’ve just over and over and over again expanded the capability of the products that were already in our customers’ homes, and we didn’t have a way of talking about it at all. So we wanted to give it a name. We wanted people to understand that it improved over time. So as we started thinking about, “Well, how do you really do that?” I mean, the first thing they came to was like, “Gosh, we already have ‘OS’ right in the name of the company.” So to try to brand some entirely new thing felt like a self-own, and so we pretty quickly reverted to this idea that the product is 100 percent hardware and 100 percent software. The product is Sonos, not Arc. Maybe the OS2 is called Sonos. And then it’s like, “Well, what companion moniker can we give it to drive that idea home?” Obviously we talked about version numbers and any number of other things, but we decided that we’d like to take advantage of this. Once a year, we’ll stand up in front of our audience and tell them about how the platform is evolving, and using the year as a descriptor for that was a better mechanism than almost anything else that was available to us. Those software cycles, yearly big releases… Apple is basically the only company that sticks to it. Even if it doesn’t do it, it announces all the features, and then they slowly dribble them out over the course of the year as they come into focus. Again, I have a lot of questions about the nature of software development in 2027, but the idea that there’s one monolithic release once a year — the industry’s gotten away from that. Is that your idea, or are you going to do something different? No, no, no. And I think having an out-loud audio experience in your home is something that everybody should enjoy. A running joke about the first generation of assistants is that they’ve ended up being for timers. With the first-generation assistants, you just had to guess what to say every time, and even then maybe it took two or three attempts to get there. It’s such a game changer to be able to say, “Play that album I loved in college, the one with the Cocteau Twins singer. I think the song ended up as the theme song for that show. It was on Fox. It was about the curmudgeonly Doctor House… It’s ‘Teardrop’ by Massive Attack.” I know. They took it off the [streaming version of House]. It’s sad. It is. It is. So that’s job one, if you like. The first-generation assistants didn’t do it at all. Sonos Voice in the new OS is exceptional at this. So that’s meeting the customer where they are. What else should they say? Well, they say a little bit about what I would call mood and ambiance. So lighting. Let’s bring lighting into the environment so that you can express a scene through both sound and light. My wife and I are constantly settling little bets in the kitchen: “What was this thing called? Who is that actor?” They do want comprehensive world knowledge. They want to get an answer to the kid’s homework assignment or whatever, and so these agents do need to be able to hand off to comprehensive frontier models to get perspectives on the world. In some instances, those perspectives allow them to use the frontier model to manipulate the audio environment to play some music that people are talking about on TikTok and pick some stuff that sounds like the shoegaze music that I liked when I was in college. We can leverage the power of a frontier model to understand what’s being talked about on TikTok to sort that against my musical preferences, and deliver it. I guess while we’re enumerating what I think a modern voice assistant has to do, it has to be fast, it has to be reliable, and it has to be responsible and affordable to run. What I really mean is that we think there’s a lot of utility in showing up inside of ChatGPT on the web, on your phone, on the desktop in the same way that Slack or your calendar shows up in those environments. By connecting that tool into your experience, however you access it, you’ll be able to summon Sonos. So let’s now talk about on-device, though. So we’ve been working closely with Amazon for some time to bring Alexa Plus to our platform. That work continues. I’m excited about it. But one thing we’ve demonstrated is something we call Sonos Custom Agents, which lets you take any of these inference engines — the Anthropic engine, the OpenAI engine, the Gemini engine, the Grok engine — and invoke it from Sonos. You’ll be able to give it a unique voice, give it a name, and give it a context that has it show up in a particular way. So imagine that you’d like to have a personality that lives inside of Sonos, and it’s the John Cusack character from High Fidelity, an opinionated record store geek who is interested in subtly shading your perspectives in music, but also making recommendations. Let’s use this voice I pick, and let’s give it this personality, and let’s have it live alongside an agent that’s powered by Anthropic inference that is going to give me advice on cooking. And let’s do another one that is only interested in connecting me with opportunities to see live music.” All of these tools will be able to show up inside of Sonos, invoked by the name you give it, like, “Hey, Sonos, connect me to the curator,” and talk to you only about that topic, have a point of view, and be able to access the Sonos context so that they can hand off from one to the other. It’s super fun. It’s super creative. It lets our customers become participants in the Sonos platform. When I asked where the computer is, this is, to me, the big question: where does all this ambient agentic AI actually live? Where does it have control? Is it going to happen locally? So one thing that I’m reminded of in this moment is that we, as an industry, have these platform shifts that unlock opportunities for companies who get lucky on timing. For example, the original dot-com era was really powered by Oracle and Sun Solaris boxes and data centers that you owned and operated yourself. We then went through a chapter where the infrastructure became Linux and open source for your data tier. Of course, we’ve had this huge long run of cloud infrastructure. I think that if you’re a company that builds your stack on Solaris and Oracle, it’s really that there’s an opportunity for a company to come along and build a stack on Linux and Postgres in that moment and suddenly find efficiencies that are very, very hard for you to claim. I think something very similar is happening in voice assistants. When I hear you talk on the show about your experience with voice assistants that are going through the transition from first generation to second generation, it feels to me like so much of the challenge there is about how we drag along all of the committed relationships that we have for the internet-connected toaster, back massager, and all of the things that are connected into those existing historical ecosystems. Honestly, even in the world of AI, with just how much has changed in the last couple of years underneath the companies as they’re building out their answers here, it really feels to me like we are stepping into a moment where agentic tool use powered by protocols, like MCP, is going to disintermediate a lot of that stuff that you’re talking about. I think that in the immediate term, we’re really excited about what we can do in the use cases that our customers are telling us they care about. Give me mood and audio in the home. We have great solutions for that today. And I think we can take advantage of this shift to a truly next-generation architecture without the baggage of a decade of internet-connected light switches from 17 different protocols with 17 different hubs in the closet. Well, I mean, part of the opportunity there is that you don’t have a big voice assistant. I don’t think the current Sonos voice assistant is at the scale of Alexa or Google Assistant. The promise of AI, at least from my very abstract vantage point, is, boy, I should not care about any of this. There’s a network in my house. There are lots of devices on that network. They have some access points on that network, and maybe they’re API-driven access points, maybe they’re MCP-driven. It should not matter.
